A Study of 50,000 Children Reveals the Real Impact of Daily Iron Pills

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A major scientific review involving more than 50,000 children and adolescents reveals that daily iron supplementation can significantly reduce the risk of iron deficiency anemia.

A daily iron supplement can substantially reduce the risk of iron deficiency anemia in children and teenagers, but it does not appear to improve growth or protect against common illnesses. These findings come from a major Cochrane review of 114 studies involving more than 50,000 participants.

Led by researchers at Cornell University’s Joan Klein Jacobs Center for Precision Nutrition and Health, the review examined daily iron supplements taken alone or combined with folic acid and other vitamins and minerals. Many of the studies were conducted in Asia and involved children younger than five.

The strongest evidence showed that iron alone probably reduces the risk of iron deficiency anemia compared with receiving no treatment or a placebo. Combining iron with folic acid probably offers similar protection, although that conclusion was based on just one study. Supplements containing iron alongside other vitamins and minerals may also help. Evidence suggests that iron supplementation can reduce anemia and iron deficiency more broadly, but confidence in several findings remains limited.

Iron is essential for producing hemoglobin, the protein in red blood cells that transports oxygen from the lungs throughout the body. When iron levels become too low, the body cannot produce enough healthy hemoglobin, potentially leading to iron deficiency anemia.

“Iron deficiency remains one of the most common nutritional problems affecting children worldwide,” says first author Dr Kripa Rajagopalan. “Our review shows that daily iron supplements do what they are meant to do, which is reduce iron deficiency anemia, without any measurable impact on the common illnesses parents and health workers worry about.”

Although iron supplements helped prevent iron deficiency anemia, the researchers found no improvement in children’s height or weight relative to others of the same age and sex. Iron alone also had no measurable effect on the occurrence of cough, fever, or respiratory illness.

Adding folic acid to iron made little to no difference in rates of diarrhea or respiratory illness. Results for other supplement combinations were less conclusive, with evidence ranging from moderate to very low certainty.

Daily iron supplementation may cause stool discoloration, but iron alone appears to have little or no effect on vomiting or diarrhea. Its relationship with constipation remains uncertain. When iron was included in supplements containing other vitamins and minerals, vomiting was probably more common than with otherwise identical supplements that lacked iron.

“While the evidence base supports the use of daily iron supplements where needed, some adverse effects do occur. Stool discoloration and, in some formulations, vomiting were more common with iron, and the certainty of evidence for several outcomes, including anemia and iron deficiency themselves, was low or very low,” explains senior author Dr Saurabh Mehta.

Uncertainty about longer-term outcomes has persisted for years. The World Health Organization’s guidelines, last updated in 2016, identified gaps in evidence concerning the long-term effects of iron supplementation in children. The new review brings together a much larger body of research, although the strength of the evidence still varies across outcomes.

Anemia affects an estimated 874 million children and women worldwide. Young children face a particularly heavy burden, with approximately 40% of children younger than five affected in 2019. Iron deficiency accounts for roughly half of these cases.

Preventing anemia is a central objective of the global health strategy for women, children, and adolescents through 2030. Iron supplementation is already widely used to prevent and treat anemia, particularly among adolescents and women in populations where iron deficiency is common.

“These findings give decision-makers a stronger evidence base for guidance on iron supplementation in children and adolescents,” says co-author Dr Julia L. Finkelstein. “The data supports continued use of iron supplementation where iron deficiency is a concern.”

The researchers caution that their findings do not justify giving every child or adolescent a daily iron tablet. Decisions about supplementation should account for individual nutritional needs, local rates of iron deficiency and anemia, and the specific supplement formulation being used.

Reference: “Daily iron supplementation for prevention or treatment of iron‐deficiency anaemia in infants, children, and adolescents” by Kripa Rajagopalan, Laura Hackl, Juan Pablo Peña-Rosas, Maria Nieves Garcia-Casal, Mochammad Rizal, Neel H Mehta, Michael Roudik, Saiful Islam, Meghan M Trumbull-Kennedy, Nabila Reem Khondakar, Juan VA Franco, Julia L Finkelstein and Saurabh Mehta, 17 November 2026, Cochrane Database of Systematic Reviews. DOI: 10.1002/14651858.CD013227.pub2

Office light may throw off the body’s daily rhythm

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In a study of day-active rats, an hour of simulated daylight at noon helped restore activity rhythms weakened by fluorescent office lighting.

People spend about 90% of their time indoors, under ceiling lights that are a small fraction as bright as the sky outside. The body reads that light as a biological signal.

In a rodent that keeps human hours, living under standard fluorescent office light weakened the animal’s daily rhythm of activity.

An hour of simulated daylight at noon each day, roughly analogous to a lunchtime walk outdoors, partly restored the rats’ daily activity rhythm.

In a second experiment, more of the male rats that spent six weeks under fluorescent light showed diabetic-range blood sugar than rats under daylight-strength light.

A smaller share of sperm were moving in the fluorescent-light group, about 19% compared with 45% under simulated daylight.

The study is small, with eight animals in the first experiment and 20 in the second, and all of them were male.

Lily Yan, a professor in the Department of Psychology at Michigan State University, led the work. The lead author is Antony Bum Kim of the Department of Architecture at the University of California, Berkeley.

“It is a biological signal that influences circadian organization and, potentially, a range of downstream health outcomes,” Yan said of light.

The body’s internal clock helps regulate daily patterns, known as circadian rhythms, in sleep, appetite, and much else.

Nearly every lab rodent is awake at night, which makes it a poor stand-in for a person whose body clock runs on daylight.

The Nile grass rat, a small African rodent, is active in the daytime, and Yan’s lab has used it for years to study how light affects the brain.

Over each cage, the team installed a programmable light that can imitate the color mix of many sources. Its daylight setting ran at about 5,600 lux, roughly the level that building designers treat as true daylight.

The office setting imitated a standard fluorescent tube at about 150 lux, the brightness of a typical indoor workspace.

Eight male grass rats lived under four lighting schedules in turn, two weeks each. Sensors logged their movement day and night.

The first schedule was steady daylight from dawn to dusk. The second was daylight that brightened to the same midday peak and faded again, which delivered about half the total light.

The third was fluorescent office light all day. The fourth was office light with one hour of full daylight at noon.

The rats were most active, and most sharply divided between a busy day and a quiet night, under steady daylight.

Under fluorescent light their activity was lower and their rhythm flatter, with its peak shifted later in the day.

The ramped daylight did not match the steady version even though it reached the same peak.

The authors wrote that the total amount of light over the day, more than its brightest moment, seems to set the strength of the rhythm.

Adding the noon hour of daylight to the office schedule raised the strength of the rhythm again, though only partly.

All eight animals showed a burst of activity during that hour. The authors called it a partial rescue.

A separate group of 20 male grass rats lived for six weeks under either steady daylight or fluorescent light, 10 in each group.

The rats’ body weight, belly fat, and average blood sugar did not differ between the groups.

But five of the 20 animals had blood sugar in a range the lab treats as diabetic, and four of the five had lived under fluorescent light.

In a follow-up with a different way of drawing blood, the pattern held. Four of eight rats under fluorescent light crossed that line, compared with one of seven under daylight.

The two groups produced the same number of sperm. Under daylight, about 45% of those sperm were moving. Under fluorescent light, about 19% were.

“Factors affecting health are not limited to drugs, diet and exercise,” Kim said. “The light environments we create in our homes, schools and workplaces are also biological exposures.”

Ten animals per group is a small sample. More rats under fluorescent light crossed the study’s threshold for diabetic-range blood sugar, while average blood sugar was similar in the two groups.

The authors also wrote that blood drawn after euthanasia can read differently, which is why they ran the follow-up.

Every animal was male, so the study says nothing about females. And a grass rat under a programmable lamp is not an office worker with windows, a commute, and a phone.

The authors wrote that the findings support daylight deficiency as a risk factor for diabetes in this animal.

The sperm result is new, they wrote, since earlier work on light and sperm looked at day length rather than light quality.

What remains untested is whether the same shifts happen in people. The authors wrote that the next step is for other groups to repeat the work in grass rats or other day-active animals.

The next question is whether an hour of brighter light at midday could help people who work far from a window maintain a stronger daily rhythm.

The full study was published in the journal Journal of Biological Rhythms.

‘They Can Take My Stage’: Macklemore Teases New Song Benefiting Palestinian Mothers & Children

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

"They can't take my faith/ When love is the call," he raps on "Where's Our Love."

Macklemore is continuing his fight for peace in Gaza with a new song called “Where’s Our Love” that will benefit Palestinian mothers and children.

The rapper announced Wednesday (Nov. 18) that the track would drop on streaming services Friday (Nov. 20), sharing a song and music video teaser. Over stirring, reflective piano and clips of both Palestinian suffering and resilience in Gaza, Macklemore spits, “We circled the sun three times/ Have you heard the olive tree cry?/ What have we done?/ Where’s our love?”

The lyric refers to the three years of violence that have unfolded since the Oct. 7, 2023, attacks, in which terrorist organization Hamas killed approximately 1,200 Israelis. More than 74,000 Palestinians have died in Israel’s ensuing war on Hamas, according to Gaza’s Health Ministry via the Associated Press, and the United Nations has classified Israel’s actions as a genocide, something the country and its ally United States deny.

“All proceeds will go to the Mother of Hind Fund, to support mothers and children in Gaza,” Macklemore announced in his caption.

The musician has been one of the most outspoken artists in the stance that Israel is committing genocide, and incorporates “Free Palestine” demonstrations into his performances. This led to his dismissal from Ed Sheeran’s Loop Tour earlier this year as stadium owners such as Robert Kraft — who owns Gillette Stadium — threatened to cancel their scheduled concert dates unless the British pop star’s team dropped the rapper from the lineup.

Macklemore went on to launch his own Free Palestine Tour, selling out arenas in Dublin, Paris and London. When he tried to keep the trek going in the U.S., however, he alleged that multiple venues had more or less turned him down.

“They can take my stage/ They can taint the cause,” he raps on “Where’s Our Love.” “They can’t take my faith/ When love is the call.”

The “Thrift Shop” artist has previously released music in support of Palestinians, dropping “Hind’s Hall” in 2024 and “F–ked Up” in 2025.

Watch the teaser for “Where’s Our Love” below.

The "shameful" measles data that lost the US its elimination status

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Measles became endemic in the US months ago—and it’s only accelerating.

International health authorities formally announced on Tuesday that the US has lost its measles elimination status, a hard-fought public health triumph won in 2000 after decades-long vaccination efforts. The loss had been widely expected by public health experts for months, and the decision was leaked by the press last week. Still, having the designation officially stripped stings.

After the announcement Tuesday, public health experts, doctors, and political leaders decried the dramatic backslide for the US, calling it “shameful” and a “national embarrassment.” News articles point to the staggering vertical rise of measles cases this year, currently over 5,400 cases, more than any other year since 1991. An explosive measles outbreak in Pennsylvania accounts for over 1,500 of those cases. There have been 10 confirmed deaths across three states, several deaths just in the last week.

But the measles data that caused the US to lose its status—the bits of it that are public at least—paint a darker picture of where the US is in its rematch with measles, the most contagious virus known on Earth. For one thing, almost none of the data from this year—the cases, the explosive Pennsylvania outbreak, the deaths—factored in to the loss. It was lost earlier. What’s happening now is a runaway epidemic.

The US measles elimination status is a designation granted by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), which is a regional division of the World Health Organization. Last week a committee of experts assembled by the PAHO, called the RVC, met to review measles data for countries across the region, including the US. (RVC is short for the Measles, Rubella and Congenital Rubella Syndrome Elimination Regional Monitoring and Re-Verification Commission.)

For their review of the US data, the RVC heard from an independent committee of experts from the US, called the NSC, which presented measles data collected from January 2025 onward. (The NSC is short for the US National Sustainability Committee for the Elimination of Measles, Rubella, and Congenital Rubella Syndrome.)

According to the PAHO, an elimination status is lost when epidemiological and laboratory data shows that a single viral lineage has spread continuously for at least 12 months within a country. At this point, the virus is considered to be reestablished or endemic. So, to withdraw the US elimination status, the RVC needed to see evidence that a single introduction of a measles virus led to a 12-month-long chain of cases, which requires a lot of data.

The full set of data the NSC presented to the RVC is private for now. The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention needs to review and approve such data for public release, which can be a lengthy process that has not yet occurred.

The Department of Health and Human Services, which oversees the CDC, did not respond to a question from Ars Technica regarding when the data may be released.

But Ars Technica spoke with the chair of the NSC, Noel Brewer, who tried to talk through the pieces of data in the NSC report to the PAHO that he could discuss—these are pieces that have already been made public at some point by others, such as by state health officials or researchers. Brewer said the NSC presented the PAHO with genetic data spanning January 2025 to January 2026, and also epidemiological data that extended to June 2026.

In a press briefing Tuesday, officials for the PAHO made clear that only some of the data presented by NSC factored into its assessment of the US elimination status. The RVC only looked at the reporting period between January 2025 and January 2026, which doesn’t include the massive outbreak ongoing in Pennsylvania, which began in April. “The number of cases in Pennsylvania did not actually contribute to the decision that was made by the Reverification Commission,” Rhonda Sealey-Thomas, PAHO’s assistant director, explicitly told reporters Tuesday. For 2025, the US reported 2,289 cases nationally.

Still, there was no ambiguity about the US losing its status; “multiple lines of data show clearly that the US has sustained transmission of measles,” Brewer told Ars.

Brewer, who is also the Gillings Distinguished Professor in Public Health at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, confirmed that the beginning of the end of US elimination was January 20, 2025, in Texas—that date was included in the letter PAHO sent to the US informing it of the loss of elimination status. That Texas outbreak was declared over on August 18, 2025, after 762 cases were confirmed in the state. But the virus carried on elsewhere and is still simmering.

“Since January 20, 2025, the US, Canada, and Mexico have been passing measles cases back and forth across borders,” Brewer said, and the national data shows multiple, “genetically distinct” measles introductions into the US. “However, there is one genomic lineage that predominates across the cases and ties many of them, the majority of them, together,” he said.

For people closely tracking the US measles status, a key question has been whether the big outbreaks that followed the initial one in Texas were clearly linked. If they were, it would be immediately obvious that the US had lost the elimination status; but if not, and the outbreaks were driven by different virus introductions, the US could have kept its measles-free status based on the technicality of how the status is defined.

Data that might hold the answer to this question has been elusive. The measles strain in Texas, D8-9171, appeared similar to the viruses seen subsequently in big outbreaks in Arizona and Utah. But the strain was also similar to those from Mexico and Canada. Generally, there can be relatively small genetic changes among measles viruses, and genetic data alone is not used by PAHO to evaluate elimination. In June, ProPublica tried to get at the answer itself, conducting its own genetic analysis of measles strains in the US. It concluded that “the measles virus still spreading in Utah as of this May is very closely related to the one that sickened Texans over a year ago.” But the outlet acknowledged this wasn’t a “smoking gun.”

To date, the question lingers. Brewer cannot yet explicitly disclose what the NSC reported to the PAHO about possible links between the Texas outbreak and any of the subsequent large outbreaks in 2025. That includes the one in nearby Utah or one in South Carolina, which began in October 2025 and ultimately tallied 997 cases by the time it was declared over in April 2026.

For its part, the PAHO did not respond to multiple requests for comment from Ars Technica on linkages between the outbreaks.

But in a twist, Brewer noted that the answer to the question might be moot; the lineage that started in Texas on January 20 might not be the only one that has been sustained and could have lost the country its status.  There are “one or two that have gone on for more than 12 months,” he said.

With the designation now formally lost, health and medical experts are coming to grips with the reality that measles is once again endemic in the US. Details of how the US should move forward are up for debate; the US is now required to submit a “corrective action plan” to the PAHO, outlining how the country intends to regain its elimination status. But the fundamental solution is obvious and straightforward: The US needs to increase vaccination rates.

While the virus has been raging in religious communities with low vaccination rates, the entire US has seen slipping vaccination coverage. That makes it easier for the virus to gain a foothold virtually anywhere.

A protective vaccination rate is considered to be 95 percent; according to the latest data, only 92.4 percent of kindergartners were vaccinated with the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine in the 2025–2026 school year. Only 10 states have MMR vaccination rates at or above the 95 percent threshold. Meanwhile, non-medical exemptions from school vaccination requirements have never been higher, at 4 percent. Currently, 24 states have exemption rates at or above 5 percent, meaning that even if they are able to vaccinate every single non-exempt child, they would still not meet the 95 percent target. And this is just the data for young children. The vaccination coverage for adults is another concern, and the data there is scarce. In Pennsylvania’s massive outbreak, 1,055 of the 1,561 cases this year have been in adults ages 18 and older (less than 1 percent of the cases were fully vaccinated).

While some health officials have noted optimistically that 92.4 percent in children is just a few percentage points from the target, the reality of bringing up vaccination rates is more onerous.

PAHO Director Jarbas Barbosa stressed the point in the press briefing Tuesday: It’s “not the national average or the average in the state or even the average in a big city, you need to guarantee that in every community, village, you have this vaccination coverage up to 95 percent. This is very important,” Barbosa said.

The point has been demonstrated in the US. In 2019, the US came very close to losing its measles elimination status. Unlike now—when the virus is on a nationwide rampage, with 47 states and jurisdictions reporting cases—in 2019, the US nearly lost its status from a single, large outbreak that spread in New York. Though it didn’t explode on a national level, it took 11 months to stamp out in those undervaccinated communities. In that year, the US reported 1,274 measles cases total.

The situation in the US has only gotten worse, and the outlook is bleak, Brewer warns. “The measles outbreaks in Canada and Mexico are winding down,” he noted. “However, in the US, our measles problem continues to accelerate. There is no end in sight.”

Anti-Monuments to Trump

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

How do we memorialize the Trump era? Plus, an obituary for the National Historic Preservation Act, and having a beer with Outarra Watts.

When Donald Trump won the 2024 presidential election, I was in the last place I wanted to be: Washington, DC. Where would I go? I wanted nothing to do with his administration’s empty grandeur. I spent the day at Maya Lin’s Vietnam War Memorial, a dark wound in the earth. In this much more hopeful moment post-midterms, Ed Simon asks a related question: How should memorials remember the MAGA era? He points to historical anti-fascist examples in Germany — and also suggests the bulldozing of Trump’s ballroom to rubble as its own kind of monument.

Satire’s not a bad strategy to record this era, either. Also today, Greg Werkheiser pens an obituary for the National Historic Preservation Act. The act’s next of kin, the National Register of Historic Places, he writes, “is said to be concerned about its own health.”

Finally, don’t miss the newest installation of Jennifer Samet’s “Beer With a Painter” column. She interviews Ivorian painter Ouattara Watts, who works only at night, when the veil between this world and the next is thinnest. Plus, The Met and the Shanghai Museum are teaming up, just weeks after MoMA, Centre Pompidou, and M+ Museum joined forces. They say art imitates life; I just didn’t know that meant we were getting museum oligopolies.

How to Plan Your Holiday Meals in a Year of Unprecedented Recalls

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Following a series of high-profile food recalls and outbreaks this year, many Americans may be concerned about keeping their families safe from foodborne illness during the holidays.

In fact, according to a recent national survey by Orlando Health, around 60% of U.S. adults say they’re more concerned about foodborne illness this year than in previous years.

Although several major foodborne illness outbreaks have ended and their associated recalls are no longer active, food safety remains an important concern. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) estimates that 48 million people in the United States experience foodborne illness each year.

However, there are simple steps you can take to reduce the risk of foodborne illness when preparing holiday meals and storing leftovers.

While there were several food recalls in the United States over the summer, two of the largest involved Salmonella linked to jalapeños and Cyclospora linked to lettuce.

These outbreaks prompted widespread recalls and raised concerns about the safety of certain foods, particularly fresh produce.

Although health officials have since declared the major outbreaks over, some consumers may still be hesitant to purchase foods associated with previous recalls.

Even with this declaration, you may still feel nervous about purchasing certain foods, but experts say you don’t need to worry.

“Here in the United States, we have the safest food supply in the world. The recent recalls show that our food safety system is working,” said William Schaffner, MD, Professor, Division of Infectious Diseases at Vanderbilt University.

Michelle Routhenstein, preventive cardiology dietitian at Entirely Nourished, told Healthline that past recalls do not mean entire food categories need to be avoided. The important thing is whether the specific product you have matches a current recall, based on details such as brand, lot number, package size, and expiration date.

“You should enjoy familiar foods, including properly cooked poultry, washed fruits and vegetables, and pasteurized dairy products,” she said. “The key is checking relevant recalls and practicing safe food handling.”

“Before shopping or cooking, review FDA and USDA (U.S. Department of Agriculture) recall information and compare any relevant notices with the labels on your ingredients,” said Routhenstein.

Check the pantry and freezer, too: something purchased months ago may still be affected.

“Stay alert to any news reports about food recalls and use good food preparation practices. Those simple actions should keep your holidays happy and safe,” said Schaffner.

Consumers can sign up for food recall alerts through the FDA or visit FoodSafety.gov for recall information from both the FDA and USDA.

Schaffner emphasized the importance of following safe food-handling practices when preparing everyday meals and holiday gatherings.

“Remember the longstanding advice: keep food hot or cold. Avoid long times at room temperature,” he said.

Routhenstein advised focusing on four basics: clean, separate, cook, and chill.

Schaffner noted the importance of refrigerating leftovers promptly rather than leaving them at room temperature for extended periods. He recommends clearing the table and refrigerating leftovers soon after the meal ends.

Routhenstein reiterated that you should refrigerate leftovers within 2 hours rather than leaving them out to cool.

Divide turkey into smaller portions and transfer sides and gravy into shallow containers to help them cool quickly, she advised.

Leftovers can typically be stored in the refrigerator for 3–4 days. If you don’t plan to eat them within that time, consider freezing them.

The important thing is to keep an eye out for any new recalls, practice food safety, and enjoy your holiday meals!

"Boston's one of those cities where we've always been excited to go," Bauers said as the Red Sox introduced him on Tuesday, noting that his mother-in-law has run the Boston Marathon. "Now that we get the chance to put down roots here, call this place home, we're extremely grateful."

The Red Sox finalized a four-year, $58 million deal with Bauers that rewards the first baseman for a breakout season that included being named MVP of the National League Championship Series.

Bauers won't be playing first base full time for the Red Sox, who have Willson Contreras at the position.

The left-handed-hitting Bauers, 31, set career highs with a .266 batting average, .874 OPS, 26 home runs and 86 RBIs.

He was a postseason star, including in Milwaukee's NLCS sweep of the Los Angeles Dodgers. He hit a go-ahead homer off two-time Cy Young Award winner Blake Snell to end an 11-pitch at-bat in a 6-2 victory in Game 3 at Los Angeles.

"There's not doubt that he had many options and the fact that he chose to further his career with us is special and we're excited," Breslow said.

Added Bauers: "Winning a championship was an addicting feeling, and so I think over the next four years, the goal is to bring a World Series back to Boston."

Bauers played for Tampa Bay, Cleveland, Seattle and the New York Yankees before finding a home with Milwaukee in 2024. His .235 batting average in 2025 was his career high before his 2026 breakout.

Bauers was drafted in 2013 by the San Diego Padres. He hit .202 for the Yankees in 2023 and batted .199 the following season with Milwaukee. He considered ending his playing career before learning his wife was pregnant. He says he returned because the family needed health insurance.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/pharma/overtons-fda-nomination-has-been-pulled-white-house" hreflang="en">Heidi Overton’s FDA nomination pulled by White House</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Heidi Overton’s FDA nomination pulled by White House" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The White House pulled the nomination for Heidi Overton, M.D., Ph.D., to lead the FDA just hours before the Senate was scheduled to vote on her confirmation.

The move hardly comes as a surprise after Overton’s confirmation hearing before the Senate Health, Education, Labor and Pensions (HELP) Committee drew contention.

The news was first reported by Axios on Tuesday evening. The White House confirmed the news to Fierce the following morning.

In September, the HELP committee questioned Overton in a hearing marked by tightrope-walking on issues like vaccines, abortions, flavored vapes and whether or not President Donald Trump is capable of being wrong.

Overton conceded multiple times during the hearing that vaccines approved by the FDA, as well as the “abortion pill” mifepristone, are safe and effective. But she stopped short of admitting that Trump has been wrong when dispensing medical information out of line with what scientific evidence shows.

Overton’s nomination faced mounting pushback and uncertainty from senators ahead of a planned HELP Committee vote on Nov. 18. Fierce reached out to Sen. Ed Markey, the veteran Democratic lawmaker from Massachusetts, ahead of the vote, and he told Fierce that he would “vote against her nomination, and I urge my colleagues to do the same.”

“The FDA needs a leader who will stand up for science and for our kids’ safety, not one who stood by while both were undermined,” he added.

Republican Sen. Bill Cassidy of Louisiana, who chairs the Senate health committee, and other Republicans had also raised questions about Overton's nomination.

As support for Overton looked thin, Republicans were discussing Sen. Roger Marshall, a Kansas Republican, as a potential replacement, Semafor reported today. Health Secretary Kennedy reportedly met with Marshall in recent days to discuss filling the role, according to Politico, but the White House told the publication that it is not considering him.

Robert Steinbrook, M.D., health research group director for Public Citizen, commented today urging the HELP committee to reject Overton’s nomination: “The FDA needs a strong commissioner who stands for science and the integrity of the agency,” Steinbrook said. “Overton is the wrong choice for FDA Commissioner and has done nothing to dispel the well-founded reasons to oppose her candidacy.”

Analysts from Citi shared that the White House’s decision to pull the nomination has a negative impact on biopharma, “extending uncertainty around permanent agency leadership.”

“We view the withdrawal as a modest sentiment negative, but not a material near-term change to the regulatory environment facing most drug and biologics sponsors,” the analysts said in the note.

The withdrawal of Overton's nomination leaves a gap at an FDA that has already faced significant upheaval. The White House only shifted its focus to Overton in August, after the agency had spent three months without a permanent leader.

Since former FDA commissioner Marty Makary, M.D., departed the agency in May, Kyle Diamantas, who hails from the agency’s food regulation division, has been at the helm in an interim capacity.

Scientists Discover a Disturbing Link Between Cannabis Use and Earlier Psychosis

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In studies where psychosis typically emerged at age 30 or later, cannabis users experienced it more than six years earlier, raising questions about cannabis use throughout adulthood.

Most discussions about cannabis and mental health focus on teenagers, but new research suggests the conversation should extend into adulthood. A large analysis found that among people who developed psychosis, cannabis users experienced their first episode an average of two and a half years earlier than non-users. The findings reveal a consistent association, although they do not prove that cannabis caused the difference.

Researchers at UNSW Sydney examined 149 studies involving more than 71,000 people, largely conducted in Europe, North America, and Australia since 2011. The analysis, published in Biological Psychiatry, focused mostly on people with schizophrenia.

Psychosis can involve hallucinations, delusions, disordered thinking, and changes in behavior. It is associated with schizophrenia and other mental illnesses and typically emerges in late adolescence or early adulthood. According to Psychosis Australia, up to 3% of adults experience a diagnosed disorder involving psychosis during their lifetime.

The researchers found that the age difference between cannabis users and non-users grew in studies where psychosis developed later in life. When the average age of onset was between 20 and 24, cannabis users experienced their first episode 1.6 years earlier. That difference increased to 4.43 years in studies with an average onset between ages 25 and 29, reaching 6.3 years when the average onset was 30 or older.

Chief investigator Matthew Large, a psychiatrist and Conjoint Professor at UNSW’s School of Clinical Medicine, said the findings suggest that the effects of cannabis use may accumulate over time.

“We’ve tended to focus on the dangers of using cannabis in adolescence, when the brain is still developing, but the risk doesn’t disappear once you’re in your 20s,” Prof. Large said. “What we’re seeing suggests a cumulative effect, where harm builds up over time, a bit like alcohol or nicotine.”

Interestingly, the researchers found no statistically significant difference in the age of psychosis onset between teenagers who used cannabis and those who did not. However, because psychosis rarely develops before the mid-teens, there is a limited window in which researchers could detect an earlier onset.

“There may be a biological floor, in that there is only so much earlier that onset can be shifted. In younger groups, strong development, genetic or other psychosis risk factors may dominate, making any cannabis-related age shift harder to detect,” Prof. Large said.

The analysis builds on previous research led by Large. A smaller 2011 meta-analysis found that psychosis began 2.7 years earlier among cannabis users, while a 2015 analysis found that psychosis typically developed 6.3 years after cannabis use began. Those earlier studies lacked sufficient data to compare different age groups.

Lead author Carly Stevens, from UNSW’s School of Biomedical Sciences, said the new age comparisons challenge the idea that delaying cannabis use until adulthood necessarily avoids its potential psychiatric risks.

“What we’ve found challenges the assumption that you’ll be fine if you wait to smoke weed until your mid-20s; that’s just not the case,” Ms Stevens said.

“There’s been so much emphasis on adolescents not smoking cannabis, which is important, but we need to broaden the warnings for everyone.”

An important limitation is that the research cannot establish whether cannabis directly causes psychosis or makes it develop earlier. Doing so would require experiments that deliberately expose people to potential harm, which Large said would be unethical. The findings also do not establish how likely an individual cannabis user is to develop psychosis.

“But the consistency of the evidence, particularly the strong effect in older adults who likely have been using cannabis for longer, does support the theory that cannabis plays a causal role in the timing of psychosis onset, and possibly the onset of some cases among people who would not have otherwise developed psychosis.”

For people who do develop psychosis, the timing of their first episode can affect important stages of life. Stevens noted that even a difference of a few years could interfere with opportunities that might otherwise shape someone’s future.

“People may miss out on study, job opportunities, travel and relationships in those years, and treatment can come with physical side effects that can make life harder as well,” she said.

Large believes reducing or stopping cannabis use could offer meaningful benefits if it helps delay psychosis, although the analysis did not directly test that possibility.

“It’s a major illness, which robs you of your shared reality with family, friends and all those around you,” he said. “If we can delay psychosis by even a few years, people will be more likely to reach crucial milestones in education and training, and develop strong support networks, which can protect against psychosocial decline.”

Delaying the onset of psychosis could also ease pressure on healthcare systems. According to Large, psychotic disorders account for up to 3% of health-related expenditure in developed nations.

The researchers believe their findings support clearer public health warnings about the potential psychiatric consequences of cannabis use, even while questions about causation remain unresolved.

“We can’t run direct experiments, so we have to go with the best evidence available,” Prof. Large said. “In my view, the threshold for health warnings about cannabis and psychosis has definitely been met.”

One concern is the expanding use of medical cannabis. Large said he has treated a growing number of people with psychosis who had been prescribed cannabis for conditions where evidence of benefit remains limited. He worries that its medical applications can lead people to underestimate potential risks.

“It’s got this therapeutic halo around it, which blinds people to the risk.”

The increasing popularity of cannabis edibles raises additional questions. Some products can contain higher concentrations of tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), the main compound responsible for cannabis’s psychoactive effects.

Large also sees opportunities for harm reduction through cannabis legalization. Although legalization could increase access, government oversight could allow authorities to regulate THC potency, improve consumer education, and introduce requirements aimed at helping buyers understand potential health risks.

“You could tax it, regulate it, and require people to show they understand the risks before buying,” Prof. Large said.

For now, the researchers want to better understand the biological mechanisms that might connect prolonged cannabis exposure with the timing of psychosis. They are calling for more research involving older cannabis users, particularly as medical cannabis and high-potency products become more widely available.

Future studies will also need clearer, standardized measures of cannabis exposure to determine how different patterns and durations of use relate to psychosis onset.

Reference: “Cannabis Use and Age-Related Acceleration of Psychosis Onset: A Meta-Analysis” by Carly Stevens, Hannah Pincham and Matthew Large, 18 August 2026, Biological Psychiatry. DOI: 10.1016/j.biopsych.2026.06.025

These Remote Atolls Are Among the Last Places on Earth Humans Haven’t Touched. Here’s What Scientists Found.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Bikar and Bokak sit so far from people that they may show us what the ocean looked like before humans.

There are few havens on Earth as pristine as the Bikar and Bokak atolls. Formed from sunken volcanoes around which coral reefs grew, these atolls in the Ratak chain of the Marshall Islands teem with life and remain uninhabited by humans. Rainbows of fish swim among corals and sea anemones, while sharks glide silently through the water in pursuit of prey. Until recently, even marine biologists had rarely explored these habitats in depth.

That began to change when Alan Friedlander set out across an impossibly blue swath of ocean and led his team into paradise. Friedlander is a marine ecologist at the University of Hawai’i who served as chief scientist for National Geographic’s Pristine Seas program on the expedition, and he takes a special interest in remote and protected regions of the ocean. The people of the Marshall Islands have known Bikar and Bokak for generations, but the atolls had never been comprehensively surveyed when Friedlander and his team arrived. For the Marshallese, the ocean is home even more than the land is. Their survival depends on the health of the fragile reef ecosystems around the country’s 1,225 islands and islets, only some of which are inhabited.

The opportunity came in 2023, when Pristine Seas was invited to join the Marshall Islands Marine Resources Authority in surveying Bikar and Bokak, which are safeguarded by the Utirik Atoll community. Friedlander and his team received a traditional blessing and farewell on Utirik before sailing north to Bikar, which lies about 72 miles (116 km) from the nearest inhabited atoll. Bokak is even more isolated, about 239 miles (385 km) away. This remoteness is part of the reason the atolls have remained relatively untouched by human influence. Friedlander wanted to carry out an ecological assessment of everything from the land habitats to the reefs and the open sea. That meant studying the structure and biodiversity of these ecosystems, documenting how well they had been preserved and how the two atolls differed, and identifying the conservation measures they would need in the future.

“The remote northern atolls of Bikar and Bokak represent some of the least disturbed coral reef ecosystems remaining in the tropical Pacific,” Friedlander and his co-authors wrote in a study recently published in Frontiers in Marine Science. “Both atolls are uninhabited and geographically isolated from major population centers and industrial development. Previous expeditions documented portions of their terrestrial habitats, seabird colonies, sea turtles, and shallow coral reefs.”

The team’s first stop was Bikar, which curves across the water like a crescent moon. There, the researchers used a variety of sampling methods, including scuba surveys, photography, underwater video, deep-sea cameras, and a submarine, to record which species were present and in what numbers. Polynesian rats, which humans introduced, were sometimes seen scurrying through the island’s old-growth Pisonia forests. Even so, native species still thrive. Seabirds such as masked boobies, great frigatebirds, black noddies, and white terns filled the skies, while shorebirds like the migratory Pacific golden plover pecked at the sand in search of small invertebrates. Bikar’s beaches are also a nesting ground for green sea turtles; the team recorded 132 nest pits there. Its reefs are full of fish, including several species of snapper and grouper. Giant clams, which host photosynthetic algae, filter the water and are vital to the health of the reef.

Farther north and even more isolated, Bokak is an oasis that has seen very little human interference. Its drier, savanna-like landscape is covered in tufts of bunchgrass and tree heliotropes, which bear clusters of small white flowers. Many of the seabirds that soar over Bikar are also found on Bokak. Bokak’s waters are even richer than Bikar’s: its reefs are dominated by coral and hold more fish by weight. Friedlander dove in to investigate. He came across a school of humphead parrotfish, a threatened species once thought to have nearly vanished from the islands. He also saw an unusual reversal of the typical food pyramid. Reef sharks and black jacks outweigh their prey in total biomass. Because these top predators feed so often, they keep energy and nutrients moving quickly through the reef, which helps the ecosystem run efficiently.

After Bokak, Friedlander’s team had one more location to survey, and it offered a sharp contrast. From 1946 to 1958, Bikini Atoll was a center of U.S. nuclear testing, starting with Operation Crossroads. When the islanders were evacuated before the first tests, they took only a few changes of clothes, not knowing that they would never be able to resettle their home for good. The series of detonations devastated the atoll. From the ocean’s glassy surface, Bikini seems to be ringed by cities of coral. But divers who swam closer found that many of these formations, which had taken thousands of years to grow, were little more than skeletons. Still, Bikini Atoll is not without hope. After searching long enough, Friedlander found patches of live coral that had settled on the dead reef and were swarming with fish. It was a sign of how resilient life can be.

Even Bikar and Bokak can’t fully escape what is happening elsewhere. Their remoteness protects them from most direct human pressures, but climate change is slowly reaching them, too. When the water gets too hot, corals expel the symbiotic algae that feed them and give them their bright colors, a process known as coral bleaching. Leftover radiation from the nuclear-testing era is still a source of contamination in the region. Meanwhile, currents carry in trash from other countries, and plastics break down into microplastics. People are responding with conservation measures. In 2025, two years after the expedition, the government of the Marshall Islands designated the waters around Bikar and Bokak, about 18,500 square miles (48,000 square km), as the country’s first national marine sanctuary, as part of the community-led Reimaanlok conservation framework. Despite the threats that come from far away, Friedlander is optimistic.

“As relatively intact tropical marine ecosystems become increasingly rare worldwide,” he and his co-authors concluded, “remote atolls such as Bikar and Bokak serve not only as regional conservation priorities but also as globally important ecological reference systems for understanding the structure and future trajectories of tropical oceanic ecosystems in the Anthropocene.”

Elizabeth Rayne is a creature who writes. Her work has appeared in Popular Mechanics, Ars Technica, SYFY WIRE, Space.com, Live Science, Den of Geek, Forbidden Futures and Collective Tales. She lurks right outside New York City with her parrot, Lestat. When not writing, she can be found drawing, playing the piano or shapeshifting.

Uncontacted Indigenous tribe reaches out for help in the Brazilian Amazon

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 17, 2026 2:08 PM EST

Around the world, the number of uncontacted Indigenous tribes continues to dwindle. Indigenous rights advocates estimate that half of the nearly 200 communities still existing in comparative isolation could disappear within a decade, without proper safeguards against climate change, land loss, fame-seeking opportunists, and illegal industrial encroachments. Unfortunately, the situation has apparently grown so dire that some Indigenous groups are reaching out for help—and exposing themselves to potentially deadly foreign diseases in the process.

On November 17, the nonprofit advocacy group Survival International reported a recent incident in which 20 members of the Awá people intentionally contacted local government officials in the Brazilian Amazon. Although proficient hunter-gatherers, the group requested yams before taking them back into the rainforest.

“This is a troubling indication of how the continued invasion of uncontacted peoples’ forests puts otherwise self-sufficient Indigenous people in real danger,” Survival International director Caroline Pearce said in a statement.

The Awá are one of the world’s last uncontacted Indigenous tribes, whose ties to the Brazilian rainforest stretch back centuries. They faced their first attacks from European settlers during the 1800s, which eventually forced them to adopt a nomadic lifestyle to avoid future encounters. Brazil finally supplied the Awá with settlement areas during the mid-1980s, but it still took years before the government properly invested in demarcated land. In the meantime, numerous deadly interactions with loggers, farmers, and other illegal intruders had reduced the Awá community to barely 300 people. Of those, advocates estimate only about one-third of the hunter-gatherers remain completely isolated from the outside world.

“The Brazilian government must commit the resources necessary to protect [the region of] Arariboia from the loggers and ranchers who are razing it, in flagrant defiance of the law,” Pearce added.

In addition to the advocacy work by Survival International, the Awá are supported by a team of Indigenous members of the Guajajara community known as the Forest Guardians. These volunteers have spent years pushing back against illegal cattle ranchers and loggers attempting to claim land in Arariboia, often dying as a result of their efforts.

“The Forest Guardians have always worked to protect the Awá against encroachment, even at the cost of losing warriors in battle,” the organization said. “We demand immediate action from the relevant authorities…to ensure what the Awá believe is best for them, safeguarding their lives, health, safety and dignity. FOR THE AWÁ, FOR THE TENETEHAR [language], AND FOR THE FOREST!”

Opinion: The problem with ‘zero tolerance for bad behavior’ signs at hospitals

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

By Autumn Fiester, Aliza Narva, and Stacy C. Smith

Fiester is professor of medical ethics and health policy at the University of Pennsylvania’s Perelman School of Medicine and director of the Penn Program in Clinical Conflict Management. Narva is the director of ethics at the Hospital of the University of Pennsylvania. Smith is a registered nurse, certified health care ethics consultant, and qualitative researcher.

Enter any hospital today and you’ll see signs warning patients and visitors that abusive language, disruptive behavior, or aggression will not be tolerated. Some threaten removal from the facility. Others warn that security or police may be called for infractions. Almost all of them declare “zero tolerance” for any of these behaviors.

The motivation behind these signs is understandable. Workplace violence against health care workers is a serious problem in U.S. hospitals, with research demonstrating an increase in frequency over the past five years.

In response to this widespread problem, both state governments and hospitals have made a concerted effort to demonstrate that they are taking this problem seriously. They have enacted a range of anti-violence measures that include reporting systems, de-escalation training programs, weapon-detection portals, increased security presence, and posted behavior signs.  Our own health system has implemented similar measures.

While these efforts are laudable in intent, not all of them are evidence-based with demonstrable efficacy in curtailing workplace violence. That includes the now-ubiquitous posted hospital behavior signs. Multiple studies that have tested these signs do not deter patients or families from committing acts of violence or reduce the frequency of physical threats.

If posted hospital behavior signs were merely ineffective at reducing workplace violence, they would be pointless, but not harmful.

But we believe that behavior signage is more problematic than it appears. The content of the signs sends clear messages to patients and families about their tenuous status and value in the institution. These signs telegraph to patients and their loved ones that they are on notice, warned that their care can be withheld and their behavior policed — even punished. In the urgency to reduce violence against health care workers, hospitals have failed to consider the message these signs communicate and whether that message aligns or conflicts with the core values of medicine: trust, care, compassion.

In our review of posted behavior signs, we found that they target only patient and visitor conduct. They rattle off a list of patient and family “do nots,” the hospital’s mechanisms of behavior enforcement, and the dire consequences of behavioral infractions. Even when the signs give a nominal nod to the institution’s commitment to reciprocal expectations for respectful communication between patients and the clinical staff, the list of “do nots” applies only to patients and families. They list no parallel behavioral standards for nurses or physicians. That makes behavior signs ignore the relational aspect of conflict. Research on lapses in clinical professionalism points to a more nuanced explanation for some of the challenging behaviors exhibited by families and patients. The Joint Commission has even named such clinical behavior as one of the causes of violence against health care workers.

Behavior signs also display from a type of institutional amnesia, forgetting why patients and families have come to the hospital in the first place: They are in pain, anxious, frightened, grieving. They may have or soon receive a life-altering diagnosis or a heartbreaking prognosis. They are vulnerable. Is this a group of people who should receive stern warnings and threats of removal as they enter the building?

When signs espouse “zero tolerance” for “noncompliant,” “disrespectful,” or “demanding” behavior, they signal to patients and their families that raising concerns about their care can be interpreted as misconduct. In a setting where patients and families already feel profound vulnerability, voicing a complaint about their clinical care can feel too risky.

That silence risks patient safety. Data show that patient and family feedback can reveal genuine safety concerns and quality issues that would fly under the radar if they remained unvoiced. Without patients and families feeling welcome to express concerns, hospitals can miss the exact information that would improve patient care and prevent patient harm.

Hospitals do not have to choose between protecting hospital staff and preserving patient/family trust in the institution.

Imagine signs that convey not threats, but shared expectations and a willingness to help when things go wrong:

“Our staff is here to help you during what may be one of the most difficult days of your life. We ask everyone, including staff, patients, and families, to treat one another with respect. If something is not going well, please let us know so we can help resolve it.”

Such messages still make clear that violence will not be tolerated. But they also clearly convey that health care is a partnership, that the experience of illness can be challenging, and that civility helps everyone. They express behavioral standards, while also indicating that the hospital’s response to challenging interactions is to help, not punish. Some hospital systems have already replaced their “zero tolerance” signs with civility signs that project care and concern.

Protecting health care workers from violence must continue to be an institutional priority, but that effort requires evidence-based prevention programs, including efforts to understand and address the root causes of conflict.

Messages about patient, family, and clinical staff behavior can be delivered in a way that acknowledges the concerns of hospital employees while also preserving patients’ and families’ sense of belonging and their ability to ask hard questions, voice concerns, and participate fully in their care.

The words we post on hospital walls must communicate more than rules. They must communicate medicine’s values.

Autumn Fiester, Ph.D., is professor of medical ethics and health policy at the University of Pennsylvania’s Perelman School of Medicine and director of the Penn Program in Clinical Conflict Management. Aliza Narva, J.D., R.N., is the director of ethics at the Hospital of the University of Pennsylvania, where she leads the hospital’s ethics committee, ethics consultation service, ethics education initiatives, and nurse leader ethics fellowship. Stacy C. Smith, Ph.D., R.N., is a registered nurse, certified health care ethics consultant, and qualitative researcher whose work focuses on conflict and communication at the bedside.

Have an opinion on this essay? Submit a letter to the editor.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/hlth-2026-glp-1-costs-remain-challenge-new-cvs-study-aims-identify-right-price" hreflang="en">HLTH 2026: GLP-1 costs remain a challenge. A new CVS study aims to identify the right price</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
HLTH 2026: GLP-1 costs remain a challenge. A new CVS study aims to identify the right price" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Cost and access to GLP-1s remains a challenge, but the benefits of these drugs aren't in doubt.

A new study from CVS Health aims to quantify at what price a GLP-1 drug has generated enough savings elsewhere through improved clinical outcomes. The healthcare giant used anonymized claims data from Aetna members in fully insured plans and found savings from reduced hospital and medical spending, as well as improved health outcomes.

But those benefits weren't enough to offset the increased pharmaceutical spending at the current price point, the study found.

The study tracked a mix of members who took GLP-1s from 2023 to 2025, so some patients had three years of follow-up care and management.

Amy Compton-Phillips, M.D., chief medical officer at CVS, told Fierce Healthcare that part of the challenge in this equation is that these drugs gained steam as a treatment for obesity at a high price. And while those costs have come down, the reductions haven't yet been enough to offset the expenditures.

Another challenge for employers in particular — where GLP-1 coverage has been most in flux — is turnover, she said. While GLP-1s do drive significant improvements, an employer may not reap the benefit of the reduced cost if an employee moves to a different company before those are realized, even though they paid for the medication initially.

The study sought to identify a price point where those savings would be felt and landed at $350 per month over a three-year period. Achieving that, Compton-Phillips said, would likely lure employers into embracing broader GLP-1 coverage.

"For them, it might be really worthwhile to say, 'Hey, at this price point, I can afford to cover this for my employees, knowing that in the near term the savings will pay for my investment,'" she said.

The study found obesity rates declining after a 2022 peak, as well as a reduction of between 34% and 42% in hospitalizations over three years. Patients also had 14% lower odds of a serious cardiac event.

For patients who remained on the drugs for three years, their total medical spending decreased by $9,400, but associated pharmacy spending increased in tandem by $13,300, the study found.

Clinical improvements also fell off when patients were not able to remain adherent to the medication over several years, highlighting the importance of a wraparound approach that supports broader care management rather than simply prescribing a patient a GLP-1.

Compton-Phillips said CVS' own weight management program brings nutrition, peer coaching and other supports into the fold, in addition to the prescription. This is especially critical, she said, as evidence suggests weight gain is common after a patient stops taking their GLP-1, making behavior change key.

"At the moment, there's not great evidence that if you come off the drugs, you will maintain the weight loss," she said.

The CVS researchers said that the ultimate goal of the study was not to demand that all GLP-1 prescriptions repay their own costs, but to ensure that drug costs align with patient benefits and the broader cost of access. By lowering the net price of these products, more people can access them, the study concludes.

"These drugs make people's lives better, and I want to give them out," Compton-Phillips said. Doing so becomes a challenge, though, "at the price they are today," she said.

Whale snot helps scientists sequence an entire genome

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 17, 2026 5:00 AM EST

Whale vomit can be turned into expensive perfume. Whale carcasses will sink to the bottom and become marine life habitats. Whale pee nourishes the entire ocean. But how about whale snot? The microbes and other biological material emitted from their blowholes when the giant cetaceans breathe has helped scientists sequence an entire whale genome: Take that whale vomit. The findings are detailed in a study published today in the journal Molecular Ecology Resources.

Whole genomes are crucial for animal conservation. With genomic data, researchers can measure population health, to see how much genetic diversity and inbreeding is present. It can also help identify individual animals for monitoring, help determine an animal’s sex, and estimate the population’s size.

To obtain genetic data from whales, marine biologists historically have had to conduct a skin biopsy. They’ll take a dart, stick it into the whale’s blubber, and take a small sample of the skin and blubber. However, during sampling, boats must get close to the whales, which can disturb them. In some locations and for some endangered species, getting close enough to take a biopsy isn’t permitted or possible.

That’s where whale snot—or blow—comes in. For this study, a team from Scotland’s University of St. Andrews, the University of Copenhagen in Denmark, the North Coast Cetacean Society, and Fisheries and Oceans Canada used several small drones off the coast of British Columbia. The drones were equipped with sterile petri dishes to collect the samples.

The team flew the drone through the exhaled breaths of one North pacific humpback whale (Megaptera novaeangliae kuzira) at a time, but still sampled several individual whales. The humpbacks generally took one to six breaths every time they surfaced—covering the drone and the dishes in whale snot.

The team collected 58 blow samples from 26 individual humpback whales. They also took repeated samples from the same whales, collected in different encounters and across two years to match the genomes. They then cross-referenced the genomes collected from the whale blow with genomes sequenced from traditional blubber biopsies taken from the same animals.

To keep the DNA from mixing, the team targeted the whale most upwind in the pod, since the blow downwind might mix with the blow from the whales up ahead. Resting whales were never sampled, and the team stopped the drone flights if a whale showed any reaction. More than one third of samples were collected by flying the drone directly from land—no boat required.

The DNA was sequenced to produce two types of genomes. Low-coverage nuclear genomes, which are inherited from both parents, and high-coverage mitochondrial genomes, which are inherited from the mother only.

The team found that the genetic diversity estimated from the blow samples was very similar to the estimates taken from traditional biopsies. Fortunately, the humpback population in the study shows little evidence of recent inbreeding. There is a small proportion of the genome that has long stretches of identical DNA, but the team believes that most likely reflects distant shared ancestry or past population bottlenecks.

According to the team, this research marks one of the first whole genome datasets for a group of North Pacific humpback whales. Previously, only one North Pacific humpback whole genome was publicly available.

“We will use these blow-derived genomes to not only assess population structure and health, but also assess kinship and family ties among humpback whales foraging in northern British Columbia,” Dr. Éadin O’Mahony, a study co-author and marine mammal ecologist who was at the University of St. Andrews at the time of the study, said in a statement. “The aim is to understand how their social bonds form: Are the whales that forage together family, or long-term friends?”

As expected, the North Pacific humpback whales were genetically distinct from their counterparts living in the North Atlantic. This confirms that data taken from whale blow works as well from data taken from conventional biopsies. The drone-captured genetic analyses also correctly determined every whale’s sex, and revealed three previously undescribed mitochondrial DNA variants, which could help show how some of these whales are related. The blow from whales swimming in groups also did not have any sign of contamination from neighboring whales.

About 16 percent of the DNA in each blow sample were also primarily from the microbes living in the whale’s airways. According to the team, this indicates that a single breath could tell us about the individual whale and its respiratory microbiome, opening up a potential new window into cetacean health.

That’s a whole lot of data from just a little bit of snot.

Mind Games: My ‘Secret’ to Enjoying Coffee Without Anxiety

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A guide to therapy, medications, and techniques for managing anxiety

Nutrition tips and tools for supporting mental well-being

Resources for finding and receiving therapy

We include products we think are useful for our readers. If you buy through links on this page, we may earn a small commission. Here’s our process

This article is part of a mental health content series called “Mind Games,” in which Healthline editor Morgan Mandriota draws on personal experience and research to challenge misconceptions, reduce stigma, review trends, and share hot takes and lessons learned about what it means to be mentally well.

Coffee and I don’t exactly have the best relationship.

My morning cup tends to give me jitters, worsen my anxiety, and keep me up until 2 a.m. if I drink it past noon. As an already anxious human, the last thing I need is to exacerbate my symptoms, even if it’s coming from a delicious source.

Over a year ago, I noticed that my anxiety was higher than usual. I was also tossing and turning and waking up at all hours of the night for weeks on end.

One night in particular, I woke up every hour for 8 hours. Naturally, I wanted to blame it on the full moon or anything other than my beloved coffee, but alas, I realized that my caffeine consumption was the most likely culprit.

Could I have aimed to set a strict drinking schedule to see if that fixed things? Sure. But instead, I decided to quit cold turkey and undergo an experiment to see how I’d feel going coffee-free for the first time in years.

Over the next 8 months, I’d swap my coffee station for a matcha setup, dabble in exotic herbal blends from my local teahouse, and become a self-proclaimed tea connoisseur. Lo and behold, my anxiety eased up, and my sleep quality improved, too.

Fast forward to 4 months ago, when I hopped back on the coffee train. But this time, I’m relying on a handy amino acid to help avoid anxiety and other unwanted side effects: L-theanine.

Health and wellness touch everyone’s life differently. This is one person’s story. The views and opinions expressed are those of the speaker and do not necessarily reflect the views or positions of Healthline Media and should not serve as a substitute for medical advice from a healthcare professional.

L-theanine is mainly found in green and black tea and one type of wild mushroom. The mental health benefits of L-theanine consumption are an emerging area of research, but one 2024 review found that consuming around 200 to 400 milligrams (mg) may help relieve symptoms in people with anxiety disorders.

Still, anecdotal reports suggest there may be benefits of adding L-theanine to coffee. Over the last 5 years, the combo has been gaining traction on Reddit and TikTok for managing caffeine-related side effects.

Despite an overwhelming amount of rave reviews online, I couldn’t find much research to support these claims, so I spoke with a neurologist to see if they had any thoughts.

“This trend has real science behind it,” said Fawad Mian, MD, who is board certified in neurology, clinical neurophysiology, and sleep medicine and the founder of Ascend Regenerative.

“Controlled studies show caffeine plus L-theanine improves focus and attention more than caffeine alone, and some suggest it softens caffeine’s side effects, like tiredness, headaches, and blood pressure spikes,” Mian said. “It smooths caffeine’s effects but doesn’t erase them, so it’s not a license to drink more coffee,” he added.

With all of this in mind, especially after realizing just how calm I was while drinking black and green teas during my coffee break, I decided to give it a try.

After about 4 months of “testing,” I’ve found that adding L-theanine to my coffee works extremely well for me.

No more anxiety, scatterbrain, shakiness, or staying up until 3 a.m. And no lifestyle overhaul needed. The supplement, which I prefer in liquid form, has become a seamless addition to my daily routine.

Every morning, I make a fresh batch of French press coffee with (strong) local beans, then I squeeze some L-theanine into my cup.

I’ve noticed that I get the usual coffee benefits like improved focus and concentration, plus a nice, calm, stable energy throughout the day.

Even if I drink it until 2 p.m., I’m fighting to keep my eyes open at night before I finally fall asleep around 10. Perhaps this is because L-theanine may also support quality sleep.

Mindful L-Theanine Drops is the liquid supplement that I use every day.

I add a one-second squeeze (which contains 200 mg of L-theanine) into my morning coffee, then I’m good to go. I even started carrying a bottle in my bag just in case I get a latte from a coffee shop on a whim.

You can also add it to a glass of water, a cup of tea, or another drink of your choosing. It’s unflavored, so it doesn’t alter the taste of your drinks. Real secret stuff.

Out of curiosity, and because I’m a natural skeptic and overthinker, I asked Mian if my positive experience could be a placebo effect, or if I’m seeing real results.

“Expectation may play a small role, but the effect is biologically plausible,” he said. “L-theanine boosts calming brain activity that offsets caffeine’s stimulation, and 200 mg matches the doses used in studies.” However, he noted that it doesn’t speed caffeine clearance, so drinking coffee only in the morning still matters for sleep quality.

While L-theanine is generally safe to consume, it’s best to speak with a healthcare professional first, especially if you’re pregnant, nursing, receiving cancer treatment, or taking blood pressure medication or sedatives.

“Choose a third-party tested brand, and if caffeine makes you anxious, cutting back is still the simplest fix,” Mian added.

So, if you’re anything like me and drinking coffee also leads to anxiety and jitters for you, I highly recommend trying this supplement once you get the green light from your doctor. It’s been working for me. It may work well for you, too.

💌 Over to you: I’d love to hear your thoughts on this. Does coffee also give you unwanted side effects? Have you tried L-theanine to remedy them? Let me know, or ask any questions about my experience by emailing me here.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/most-nonprofit-hospitals-tax-breaks-outpaced-their-community-spending-2023-lown-institue" hreflang="en">Most nonprofit hospitals' tax breaks outpaced their community spending in 2023: Lown Institute</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Most nonprofit hospitals' tax breaks outpaced their community spending in 2023: Lown Institute" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Just over half of the country’s nonprofit hospitals have charity care and community benefit investments that fall short of their tax savings, according to the latest of the Lown Institute’s analyses on more than 3,000 hospitals’ tax filings.

In 2023, the most recent year in which Internal Revenue Service Form 990s were available for review, the think tank identified $38.8 billion of industry-wide tax exemptions, or $12.8 million per hospital and 3.8% of total expenses on average. About $37.1 billion was collectively spent by the same hospitals on various community benefits, as reported in the IRS forms matched to other filings such as Medicare cost reports.

While the total exemption handily outpaces the benefits investments by Lown’s measure, the group underscored that these "fair share” deficits or surpluses also vary widely between facilities, and that some of the industry’s biggest names are on the wrong side of the divide.

Here, Mayo Clinic Hospital led the pack with a $366 million deficit, according to Lown’s analysis, followed by Tisch Hospital (-$294 million deficit), Stanford Hospital (-$255 million deficit), New York-Presbyterian Hospital (-$248 million deficit) and Massachusetts General Hospital (-$219 million deficit).

"Some of America's wealthiest hospitals fall hundreds of millions short of giving back what they receive from their communities, leaving those with fewer resources to pick up the slack," Vikas Saini, M.D., president of the Lown Institute, said in a release on the new analysis, released Tuesday.

Among those leading the latter group were North Shore University Hospital ($167 fair share surplus), Rochester General Hospital ($146 million surplus) and Englewood Hospital ($142 million surplus). Lown’s analysis includes a searchable hospital-by-hospital breakdown of the tax exemptions and community investment totals.

When splitting the 52% of deficit hospitals apart from the surplus hospitals, the former group logged about $15.5 billion above their reported community benefits—a deficit Lown said could wipe the medical debt of 12 million Americans. Fair share surplus hospitals outpaced their exemptions by $13.8 billion.

Total tax exemptions were also “very evenly distributed” among the hospitals, Lown said. The 30 hospitals, or about 1% of all hospitals, with the largest tax exemptions collectively avoided $6.8 billion, or about 18% of total exemptions. The 607 hospitals, or about 20%, accounted for three-quarters of the country’s hospital tax exemptions.

Lown’s measure of nonprofit hospitals’ tax breaks includes exemptions from federal and state corporate income taxes, state and local sales taxes, property taxes, tax-exempt bonds, tax-exempt charitable donations and federal unemployment tax exemptions. Not included are other benefits provided to many nonprofits, like Medicaid disproportionate share payments, 340B discounts or profits from indirect tax arbitrage.

Its tally of community benefit includes spending on patient financial assistance, subsidized care, payments to other nonprofits to improve health or various community health or social determinants of health investments.

Of note, it excludes Medicaid shortfall—the difference between hospitals’ costs and revenues when treating Medicaid patients—and spending on professional education and research. The hospital industry typically points to these billions of dollars of exclusions when discussing their tax breaks, with Medicaid shortfall alone accounting for nearly half of their preferred community benefit spending totals. Lown noted in its methodology that these accounting losses are often offset by other discounts and supplemental benefits and don’t always have a direct impact on community health, among other objections.

Lown said the disparities between hospitals and the outsized benefits being delivered by some are a call to amend, not eliminate, nonprofit status exemptions. The think tank accompanied its analysis with calls to increase reporting transparency and set spending targets for hospitals tied to variables such as size, exemption value, financial health and prior spending.

“Tax exemptions aren't a gift—they're an expectation, and regulators need to make sure all hospitals hold up their end of the bargain," Saini said.

Lown acknowledged the impending financial pressure hospitals will face with upcoming H.R. 1 policy changes, and said such changes should be accompanied by “short-term” financial protections.

Still, the group isn’t alone in drawing scrutiny to the tax breaks. Lawmakers and policy advisors have pointed to, and questioned the justification for, nonprofits’ exemptions when questioning health system leaders on collections policies or high prices.

Academics and researchers have also called for greater transparency or other overhauls of how community benefits are reported or calculated. Of note, a research article published earlier this month in Health Affairs reviewing hospital filings between 2013 and 2022 outlined a disconnect between different hospitals’ listed charity care eligibility thresholds and their relative spending, which more closely aligned with community need than their stated policy.

STAT+: NIAID acting head John Powers III appointed as the permanent director
