Want to Turn Back the Clock? These 10 Diets May Help You Age Better

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

You’ve got way more options than just the Mediterranean diet.

When healthy aging is the goal, it makes sense to try everything you can to make it happen. That means doing your best to exercise regularly, keep up with doctor's appointments, and eat a well-balanced diet. You have lots of options when it comes to the last one—but it can be hard to know which of the many popular eating plans is right for you. Thankfully, new research has helped narrow the choices.

The study, which was published in Nature Communications, analyzed several different diets to see how they impacted biological aging. (Biological aging is the age of your cells, while your chronological age is how old you are.) Popular eating plans like the Mediterranean diet and the DASH (Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension) diet made the cut, of course, but several lesser-known diets did too. Here’s what to know.

Meet the experts: Jessica Cording, RD, author of The Little Book of Game-Changers; Keri Gans, RD, author of The Small Change Diet; and Sonya Angelone, RD, PhD, a former spokeswoman for the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics and a San Francisco-based dietitian.

For the study, researchers analyzed data from 6,470 participants who were 30 and older. Researchers looked at some of the participants' biomarkers that correlated with biological age and compared that with information about their diets.

After analyzing the data, researchers found that healthier eating in general was linked with slower biological aging, no matter which diet people chose. Each specific eating plan offered an edge with certain perks, though. Case in point: The DASH diet (which is specifically meant to stop hypertension) had more cardiovascular benefits for biological aging, while the MIND diet (which combines the Mediterranean and DASH diets for brain health) offered cognitive perks. But researchers discovered that there were a lot of shared biological aging benefits between the different types of diets.

These are the 10 diets flagged as being good for biological aging:

All of these diets are slightly different, but there are a few common threads. "They generally emphasize fruits, vegetables, whole grains, and other nutrient-dense foods," says Keri Gans, RD, author of The Small Change Diet.

The diets also stress the importance of minimally processed foods, while limiting refined carbs, added sugars, and processed meats, according to Sonya Angelone, RD, PhD, a former spokeswoman for the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics and a San Francisco-based dietitian. "The recommended foods provide fiber, antioxidants, polyphenols, vitamins, minerals, and healthy fats that support metabolic health and help protect cells from oxidative stress and chronic inflammation," she says. Oxidative stress and chronic inflammation are both linked to a higher risk of disease, Angelone points out.

These diets also support the heart, brain, and digestive systems, says Jessica Cording, RD, author of The Little Book of Game-Changers. "All of these things factor into the aging process and somebody's quality of life as they age," she says. "When you're eating to support these different organ systems, you support your longevity and lower your risk of disease."

There are a few things to take away from the findings, according to dietitians. One is that there isn't a single eating plan that's superior to others when it comes to longevity. "It's the overall quality of what we eat consistently that matters most, rather than following a particular diet or avoiding individual foods," Gans says.

While there are plenty of diet plans out there that claim they can help you live longer, Angelone stresses this: "Healthy aging doesn’t have to include expensive foods, supplements, or restrictive diets."

Finally, the study underscores the power of nutrition in keeping you healthy. "Our genes are not our destiny," Angelone says. "We can change the expression of our genes—genomics—with nutrition and lifestyle habits."

Supporting healthy aging with the way you eat means giving your body the fuel it requires to function well, Angelone says. "Healthy aging isn’t about finding a perfect diet or miracle food or supplement," she says. "It is about consistently providing foods that supply nutrients needed by our bodies to function optimally, repair cellular damage, and protect themselves. This will impact how we age."

Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: What a Missed Deadline Taught Me About Switching Off

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

I missed a deadline last week. The piece you’re reading was supposed to be filed before I headed out on a long weekend to Italy for my wife’s birthday.

The main problem was I simply ran out of time to write the piece. An urgent stakeholder ask out of the blue, a couple of unanticipated work fire drills, and a long-standing evening social commitment earlier in the week made for a perfect storm, and something had to give.

The other problem and consequence was that I carried a chunk of guilt into the break.

It caught up with me at 6 am on the first morning, when I found myself drafting this column in my head. I should have been dozing peacefully. I continued to ruminate on my lengthy to-do list at work deep into day one as we headed from Venice into the Dolomites. My body was in Italy, but my brain was clearly still at my desk.

Switching off, particularly from work, is hard for most of us, and especially in an age of ubiquitous screens and wall-to-wall information competing for our attention. For me, that always-on reality plays a major role in the difficulties I have disconnecting from the daily grind.

I’m fortunate to love my job, and if I’m candid, I probably take a little too much of my identity from it. I hold on tight, and the phone in my pocket is an ever-present enabler.

A meme that has stuck with me over the years goes something along the lines of being an adult is staring at medium screens all day only to come home and stare at a little screen while a big screen is on in the background. The irony is not lost on me that I came across this rather withering take on modern life while scrolling on said little screen.

Regardless of the role the screen plays, psychologists who study employee well-being describe what I was missing for the early part of the weekend as “psychological detachment.” A state where you are genuinely able to not think about work while away from it. The science suggests it’s an important thing to work on.

A 2025 meta-analysis of 32 studies found that vacationing has a strong effect on well-being, and that the effect lasts longer than earlier research suggested. A second, smaller analysis of eight studies points to detaching from work, along with physical activity, as among the most helpful things to do while you’re off. In another study that tracked teachers during a two-week break, exhaustion, anxiety, and low mood all declined, while those who kept ruminating about work recovered less.

Whatever the science says, I know that for me the act of stepping away from work and being disciplined about not checking back in while I’m away is critical for my mental health and for staving off burnout. Getting out in nature and hiking or cycling or swimming keeps me present. It resets and recharges my batteries, and it tops up my creativity in a way that I find benefits what I am then able to bring back to the office.

Sure enough, as we drove deeper into the mountains on a crisp, blue-sky day, I could feel the golden autumnal hues and majestic rock formations drawing me in, closer to an elemental feeling of calm and happiness. That sense of equilibrium stayed with me all weekend, and while I’d be lying if I said my mind didn’t occasionally drift to my desk, it was fleeting, with the knowledge that all the things would be waiting for me when I got back. Just like this piece was.

With Thanksgiving on the horizon, and the holidays coming thick and fast thereafter, I’ll leave you with this bit of hard-earned advice: if your job is deeply entwined with your life and part of who you are, as mine is, time away can sometimes feel like a small act of disloyalty. It isn’t. In fact, it’s probably the most important thing you can do to remain faithful to your work ethic, and to yourself.

And if you’ll be working over the holidays, or you’re not working right now, or you don’t have the benefit of someone on hand to carry some of the load for you (shout out to all the Grannies out there), or your vacation looks very different from mine, that’s all okay. Find the pockets where and when you can: an evening, a walk, an hour in front of a screen, or better yet a book, and let work wait. Above all else, be kind to yourself. That’s how you really give yourself a break.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

“For The First Time In A While, I Feel Joy” & 59 Other Anonymous Mom Confessions

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Hope, grief, happiness, burnout — this week’s confessions have it all.

Is that... dare we say it... hope in the air?! This week’s anonymous mom confessions came in like a tidal wave. Literally, we’ve never seen so many 🌊 emojis in our inbox. Because, yes, moms wanted to talk about the midterms. Hundreds of them! Hope, relief, crying in the kitchen, feeling like they can breathe for the first time in years... along with a few who weren’t celebrating at all. We couldn’t even print a fraction of it, so please know, if you submitted one, you were in extremely plentiful company.

But what also really struck us was the sentiment underneath: So many moms feel like they’re living in the in-between.

That tracks for the midterm results, obviously. Will we actually see change once the new class gets sworn in this January? Meanwhile, families are still struggling with inflation and high gas prices and just generally being tired of being broke. This sentiment also surfaced in confessions from moms at crossroads in their relationships, whether it’s the precipice of divorce or considering starting a spicy affair. Moms are feeling it across the board, really, from parenting (the tween years are hitting some of you hard) to health (perimenopause purgatory strikes again).

One mom kind of hit the nail on the head: Her personal life and finances are pretty much in shambles, and also, for the first time in a long time, she feels joy. Both. At once. That’s where a lot of you seem to be right now.

So there’s hope, and there’s the other thing, and most weeks they’re just two wings on the same bird. If that sounds familiar, you’ll likely find a lot of validation in this week’s roundup.

Scary Mommy Confessions are a tried-and-true part of what makes our site so fun. If you want to anonymously confess, look for our weekly callouts on Instagram. And to browse past Confessions, take a look here.

Husband has been gone all week and parenting/home life has been easier with him gone.

I coached my daughter’s basketball team, and it was a ROUGH season.

I want to have an affair with a guy at my gym but don’t want to divorce my husband.

I have two managers at work who are ruining the culture, and I don’t know how to make them stop.

So thankful for the blue wave! I hope they can accomplish amazing things!

Mad that my parents’ awful parenting creeps in with my kids even after 15 years of therapy.

In reversal, American Cancer Society urges prostate cancer tests for all men

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Men aged 50–69 and at average risk should get PSA screening every two years.

In a significant update to its guidelines for prostate cancer screening, the American Cancer Society is now recommending that all men get routinely screened for prostate cancer with a PSA test.

Men at average risk should get a PSA test every two years from age 50 to 69, according to the new guidelines released Thursday.

Men considered at high risk due to race and family history should start testing earlier, at 45, and most are recommended to get tested every year. Specifically, yearly testing starting at age 45 is recommended for all Black men, who have higher rates of prostate cancer. Non-Black men should also get yearly testing starting at 45 if a close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer before age 65, or if more than one of their relatives has had prostate cancer. On the other hand, if only one close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer and it was diagnosed at age 65 or older, non-Black men in this case should start PSA screening at 45, but are recommended to get testing only every two years.

Men ages 70 to 74 should consult their doctor on continuing screening, while those 75 and up are not recommended to get screened.

The new guidance departs from ACS’s previous iteration, published in 2010, which made no universal recommendations for routine screening. Men at age 50 were recommended to use shared-decision making with their doctors on whether or not to get tested. The recommendation was similar to that from the US Preventive Services Task Force, a federal panel that regularly evaluates evidence and makes recommendations on such preventive medical care. (The USPSTF is currently working on an update to its prostate cancer screening recommendation, though it should be noted that the panel has recently been overhauled by anti-vaccine Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and new members are reportedly rife with conflicts of interest.)

In announcing the guidelines today, the ACS says its updates are based on new evidence since 2010 that shows screening saves lives, and that advances in case management can avoid the concerning harms from overdiagnosis and overtreatment. For many men with prostate cancer, their cancer will never grow or cause harm in their lifetime, while treatment can run the risk of erectile dysfunction and urinary incontinence.

In the new guidance, the ACS adds guardrails, firstly advising all men to be informed about the risks and benefits. If a PSA test comes back high—defined as 3 ng/mL or higher for men under 70 and 6.5 ng/mL or higher for those 70 and up—the ACS recommends repeating the test before taking any next steps.

If repeat testing is still high, then men should get either a prostate MRI or a validated biomarker test to assess risk. Only if there is a high suspicion of cancer—such as having a very high PSA test result—should men jump to a prostate biopsy.

For men diagnosed with prostate cancer, active surveillance should be the initial management strategy for men whose cancer appears to be at low risk of growing or spreading.

“By catching aggressive cancers with a simple PSA blood test, you open the door to highly effective treatments that protect your future,” Dr. Shanthi Sivendran, interim chief patient officer for the ACS, said in a statement. “Meanwhile, lower-risk findings can be safely managed through careful, active surveillance.”

Prostate cancer is one of the most common cancers in men and the second leading cause of cancer death in US men, the ACS notes. Nearly 334,00 men are estimated to be diagnosed with prostate cancer in 2026, and it’s estimated that over 36,320 will die from the disease.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Missing Teen Found Alive After 6 Days Alone in Australian Bushland

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A 17-year-old boy was found alive in the Australian wilderness and has been reunited with his mother after going missing for 6 days.

The boy went missing on Friday, November 6 after his mother dropped him and a friend off to go camping. William had reportedly walked a short distance away to retrieve more camping equipment and did not return, and the friend was unable to find him despite searching the area.

An extensive search was launched, and William was ultimately located when an aerial search unit spotted a tackle box in the bushland. Honing in on the area, they soon spotted William lying near a creek nearby and were able to retrieve him. After an emotional reunion between William and his mother, he was brought to the hospital for monitoring.

“Amazingly, William is in good spirits and for the most part, in good health,” Victoria police said in a press release.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/cms-pauses-hospice-home-health-medicare-enrollments-fraud-crackdown" hreflang="en">CMS extends moratorium on new Medicare enrollments for hospice, home health agencies</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
CMS extends moratorium on new Medicare enrollments for hospice, home health agencies" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration is extending its moratorium on new Medicare enrollments from hospice and home health agencies.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said Thursday that it will extend the moratorium by six more months as part of its efforts to combat fraud, waste and abuse in the Medicare program.

CMS said that the initial moratorium window has allowed the agency "to conduct targeted investigations and deploy advanced data analytics" to identify potential cases of fraud. An extension was deemed necessary to continue that effort, as the "circumstances warranting the imposition of the moratoria have not yet abated."

The agency said that hospice and home health have long been considered high risk areas for fraud.

“We’ve made real progress over the past six months, but fraud in hospice and home health persists and our work isn’t finished,” said CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., in the announcement. “Medicare beneficiaries deserve protection from these bad actors, and American taxpayers need to know the programs they rely on aren’t being bled dry by fraudsters.”

Like the original moratorium, the extension applies nationwide. However, the extension will allow currently enrolled hospice providers or home health agencies to add new practice locations to their existing enrollment in Medicare. Providers currently enrolled in Medicare will not be impacted and can continue to provide services to beneficiaries.

The Trump administration has issued a six-month moratorium on hospice and home health agencies enrolling in Medicare as part of its efforts to combat fraud.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said in an announcement on Wednesday morning that the "data-driven" decision targets a key source of fraud activity. It follows a similar announcement made earlier this year of a moratorium on durable medical equipment, prosthetics, orthotics and supplies companies.

During the six-month window, CMS said it will focus on identifying and investigating bad actors and deploying advanced analytics that help accelerate the removal of hospice and home health providers that are likely perpetrators of fraud.

CMS added that recent action under the broader White House fraud push has suspended payments to 773 hospice providers and 23 home health agencies in the Los Angeles area, totaling about $70 million in funds.

“We’ve seen systemic and deeply troubling fraud in the hospice and home health space, with bad actors exploiting some of our most vulnerable Medicare patients and stealing money from the American taxpayer,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in the agency's press release. “Today we’re shutting the door on fraud—preventing new bad actors from entering Medicare while we aggressively identify, investigate and remove those already exploiting them."

"This is about protecting patients, restoring integrity and safeguarding taxpayer dollars," Oz said.

CMS said that the moratorium will not affect providers currently enrolled in Medicare, and they can continue to offer key services to members. It will apply, however, to new applicants for participation and "certain changes in majority ownership," which CMS said can be used to "obscure control by bad actors."

The moratoria will also prevent suspected fraudsters from evading punishment by moving across state lines, CMS said.

The announcement, the agency said, builds on a number of steps CMS has taken of late to address fraud in hospice and home healthcare, including increased oversight in states with elevated risk of fraud, such as Arizona, California, Georgia, Ohio, Nevada and Texas.

CMS is also conducting hospice site visits across the country to monitor operations and potentially identify suspicious behavior. In addition, it expanded a demonstration across several states—Florida, Illinois, Oklahoma, Ohio, North Carolina and Texas—that allowed for pre- and post-claim reviews of submissions from HHAs, which may stop improper payments before they're made.

The National Alliance for Care at Home, in a statement responding to the news, said it welcomes the administration's scrutiny and appreciates that "providers will still be able to conduct face-to-face recertification visits via telehealth during the enrollment moratorium, which will help avoid unnecessary care disruptions for patients and families." Still, it said the action raises concerns over access to care in high-demand areas and urged CMS to focus its efforts on "high-fraud markets" or actors without catching the rest of the sector in its net.

“CMS must use data-driven, risk-based program integrity measures and focus resources on boots-on-the-ground surveys and enforcement of existing oversight mechanisms that root out the blatantly bad actors without potentially limiting patient access to care or punishing high-quality providers operating in good faith," Alliance CEO Jennifer Sheets said in the statement.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/no-surprises-act-idr-claims-cost-employer-plans-additional-84b-last-year-study-finds" hreflang="en">No Surprises Act IDR claims cost employer plans an additional $8.4B last year, study finds</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
No Surprises Act IDR claims cost employer plans an additional $8.4B last year, study finds" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The No Surprises Act's independent dispute resolution process cost employer plans $8.39 billion in 2025, according to a new analysis.

Researchers at the Employee Benefit Research Institute combed federal IDR data and found that these additional costs equal about 63 cents per $100 spent by employer-based insurance plans. The total includes $4.73 billion in costs incurred in the first half of the year as well as $3.66 billion in the second half of 2025.

Even when EBRI analysts used different thresholds to exclude outlier awards, such as costs that were unusually high, they still landed on an estimate of at least $8.02 billion, per the study.

The researchers note that this data doesn't necessarily paint the full picture, as it only encompasses cases that proceeded to IDR. The study doesn't include information on claims that were settled outside of arbitration, administrative costs, any changes in networks or provider rates or the cost associated with patient protections.

The study calculates additional cost by taking the IDR award amounts and subtracting the plan's initial payment and any cost-sharing from the patient.

"These new data provide a clearer picture of what health plans owe for out-of-network services beyond their initial payments, after accounting for patient cost sharing,” said Paul Fronstin, Ph.D., director of Health Benefits Research at EBRI, in the announcement. “Separating those amounts from arbitration awards allows us to measure additional plan obligations more directly."

The study also doesn't project the impact of IDR on premiums, and the researchers said that having greater insight into the administrative expenses and other ancillary costs would be necessary to understand how the No Surprises Act's processes may impact patients' costs directly.

The IDR processes have gained increasing scrutiny from health plans and plan sponsors as the number of disputes, and their associated payouts, rise. When a claim reaches the arbitration phase, the results overwhelming favor providers, as they won in 85% of disputes last year.

An analysis from Georgetown University, released in August, estimated that total costs associated with IDR reached $22 billion in 2025. There were 2.6 million disputes initiated in 2025; by comparison, federal estimates projected about 22,000 cases annually reaching the arbitration phase.

Providers have pushed back on criticism of IDR, arguing that the root cause is payers undercutting them during the opening negotiation phase by setting low qualifying payment amounts for claims.

How Kelly Rowland Keeps Her Eczema Flare-Ups Under Control During Winter

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Wintertime can bring everyone down with its colder weather and less sunlight. But for those with eczema, this time of year can wreak havoc on their skin, too.

That’s the case for Grammy-winning singer, actress, and Destiny’s Child alum Kelly Rowland, who has been living with eczema since her 20s. During the winter months, she often dreads the cold.

“[You] don’t know what the environment is going to give you—because mine is also environmental—or what the weather is going to give you,” Rowland told Healthline.

Cold weather and indoor heating can reduce humidity, drying out the skin and triggering or worsening eczema flare-ups.

“[What we know] about the skin barrier is that it really is like a gate or wall against the outside world,” Camille Howard-Verovic, MD, a dermatologist at Active Dermatology, told Healthline.

In healthy skin, the barrier controls what enters and leaves the skin. However, for people with eczema, Howard-Verovic said they have skin barrier dysfunction, which allows substances such as allergens and irritants to penetrate the skin.

Cold weather can increase transepidermal water loss, a process in which moisture escapes through the skin’s outer barrier and evaporates into the surrounding air.

“People with eczema have a tremendous amount of transepidermal water loss, so you’re just drier,” said Howard-Verovic. “[You’re] not holding onto the water as well, so that causes the itching and the rash that we feel when we have eczema.”

Rowland first noticed eczema flares behind her ears as well as on her elbows, arms, and the back of her knees.

“[It] looked like a rash, so that’s what I thought it was all this time, and I dealt with it for so long until after I had my son and then it started to get worse on my face,” she said.

She eventually went to a dermatologist who diagnosed her with moderate-to-severe eczema, a chronic inflammatory skin condition that can cause intense itching, painful skin cracking, thickened skin, and redness or swelling.

When Rowland learned that eczema can be genetic, it made sense to her.

“I remember seeing big rashes on [my mom’s] neck and her arms…so she had it, and I guess I was the lucky one,” said Rowland.

At first, she tried eliminating foods that she thought might be triggers. She also bathed in oatmeal.

“[I] literally have had a tub full of oatmeal, like literally went to the store to get store-bought fresh oats,” she said. “I tried everything, and it doesn’t help until you go talk to a doctor about it.”

At first, she tried prescription topical medications but found them cumbersome.

“I don’t have the time, if I’m being honest, and we live in a world that is moving so fast…I started buying like three tubes of whatever it was I was using and losing it; it was just too much for me,” Rowland said.

Rowland said she found relief with Ebglyss, a prescription injectable medication for moderate-to-severe atopic dermatitis. She has also partnered with Eli Lilly and Company, its manufacturer, to raise awareness about eczema.

Rowland said she’s sharing her experience to help others living with eczema, including people she’s met who have experienced greater challenges managing the condition.

“One lady was telling me about how she’s having a tough time [looking] in the mirror cause she doesn’t know who’s looking back at her, and she saw her skin before eczema and after,” said Rowland.

When she first had symptoms, she said she felt embarrassed, too.

“[Because] it was just in the middle of my arm space and…[if I] put out my hand to shake someone’s hand, you don’t know if somebody’s judgy,” she said.

Rowland hopes being open about her condition will encourage others to advocate for themselves and talk with their doctor about treatment options.

Because eczema can look different on deeper skin tones, Howard-Verovic noted that a dermatologist can help make the correct diagnosis.

“Some people may not even know that they have eczema,” she said. “We know that eczema is a rash; it can appear red on certain skin tones, and it can be grey or white on certain skin tones, so it can look a little different.”

In addition to medication, Rowland said managing stress helps reduce her eczema flare-ups.

“Before [when] I went on tour, because I was under so much stress, it flared up like crazy, like you wouldn’t believe,” she said. “We were in the back trying to figure out how to cover certain things up, and we figured some of it out, and some of it was really tricky, but what helped was when I actually just calmed down…not that it helped overnight, but it did just kind of bring the flare down.”

She said red light therapy, feeling grounded outside without her shoes on, getting vitamin D from natural sunlight, and cooking are ways she reduces ongoing stress.

“Cooking calms me when my kids aren’t here, and I have a really good [Zen] playlist on,” said Rowland. “[If] you ask me a question [in those moments], I might give you my heart; I’m just so happy.”

She also tries to cut back on sugar, which she believes triggers flares.

Another way to reduce flares, according to Howard-Verovic, is to avoid hot showers, especially in the winter.

“[We] know eczematous skin likes lukewarm water,” she said.

Because Howard-Verovic and her daughter both live with eczema, she set a rule about when it’s time to get out of the shower.

“[If] you see condensation on the bathroom mirror, you’ve spent too much time in the shower,” said Howard-Verovic. “[That] really hot, hot water feels really good in that moment…but when you get out all that water evaporates, and there’s no moisture in the atmosphere, and it dries your skin out, and now your skin is unhappy again.”

If you prefer to take baths, she said use gentle cleansers.

“[There] are bleach baths for really, really bad flare-ups,” she noted.

Diluted bleach baths are sometimes recommended for people with eczema, but they should only be used according to a healthcare professional’s instructions.

As soon as you get out of the shower or bath, Howard-Verovic said to apply a quality moisturizer to your damp skin. According to the National Eczema Association (NEA), lotions containing the following ingredients can help ease dry skin associated with eczema.

To help identify appropriate products, look for ones that have the NEA’s Seal of Acceptance.

High Fat Diet Linked to Higher Colon Cancer Risk, Early Research Shows

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

In a new study, researchers report that a high fat diet may potentially increase the risk of colon cancer.

The researchers from Arizona State University said a diet high in certain fats can cause lasting changes in intestinal stem cells that lead to the development of tumors and the possibility of colon cancer.

The study was conducted in mice, but the researchers noted changes in the DNA of intestinal stem cells that may explain their growth and survival.

The researchers published their findings on November 12 in the journal Stem Cell Reports.

The researchers said the study is important because 1 in 25 people develop colon cancer in their lifetime. In the United States, that translates into about 108,000 new cases of colon cancer every year. About 55,000 males and 55,000 females are expected to die from the disease in 2026.

Experts not involved in the study say the research provides valuable information, but they have reservations about the results, as the findings do not yet apply to humans.

“The findings provide a potential mechanism linking diet to intestinal cancer development, but we need to be cautious about translating these results to humans,” said Dalia Perelman, MS, CDE, a research dietitian at Stanford University in California.

“We already have evidence from human studies that obesity and certain dietary patterns, particularly those high in processed meats and low in fiber-rich foods, are associated with increased colorectal cancer risk,” Perelman told Healthline. “This study adds a potentially important mechanism to investigate, but it does not establish that dietary fat itself increases colorectal cancer risk in humans.”

Ketan Thanki, MD, a colorectal surgeon specializing in benign and malignant diseases of the colon, rectum, and anus at the MemorialCare Todd Cancer Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California, shared similar views.

“This study provides a better understanding at the cellular level of how a high fat diet may contribute to colorectal cancer development,” Thanki told Healthline.

“Animal studies are an important starting point for understanding biological processes, but we must be cautious about directly applying their findings to humans,” he added. “Mice and humans share many cellular and biological mechanisms, which makes these studies valuable in identifying potential pathways involved in disease development. However, human diets, genetics, metabolism, and environmental exposures are more complex.”

To reach their findings, the Arizona State researchers fed mice what the scientists described as a Western-style high fat diet for 6 months.

They then compared those results with those from mice given a low fat diet.

The researchers said the mice on the high fat diet gained more weight than the mice on the low-fat diet. They also developed more intestinal stem cells, and those cells grew at a more rapid rate.

They added that mice genetically predisposed to intestinal cancer who were fed a high-fat diet developed more and larger tumors.

The researchers noted changes in the DNA of intestinal stem cells that may help explain their increased growth and survival.

They also pointed out that stem cell behavior returned to normal after high fat diet mice were switched to a low fat diet, although some DNA changes persisted. After four weeks, approximately 18% of these changes remained.

When mice were returned to a high fat diet, the researchers said their intestinal stem cells responded more quickly and strongly than those of mice exposed to the diet for the first time.

They concluded that the study suggests that maintaining a healthy, low fat diet may help regulate intestinal stem cell growth and reduce colon cancer risk.

Kristin Kirkpatrick, RD, president of KAK Consulting and a dietitian at the Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness & Preventive Medicine in Ohio, wasn’t involved in the study but highlighted a particular aspect of the findings.

“What I found particularly interesting was that even after the mice returned to a low fat diet, some of the molecular changes persisted,” she told Healthline. “This raises questions about how our dietary patterns over time may influence long-term health.”

Experts said these new findings are interesting but noted numerous caveats.

“One important takeaway is that dietary habits over time matter,” said Thanki. “Even if someone has consumed a high fat diet for years, making changes now may still provide meaningful health benefits.”

“I also think these findings are particularly important for children and adolescents, as establishing healthier dietary habits early in life may provide long-term benefits,” he added.

However, Thanki noted that not all fats are the same. “The health risks depend partly on the type of fat consumed, how much is consumed, and what foods make up the overall diet,” he said.

“Diets consistently high in saturated fats, particularly those containing large amounts of processed meats, fried foods, and highly processed products, can contribute to obesity, elevated cholesterol, cardiovascular disease, and metabolic dysfunction. These factors can also contribute to chronic inflammation and may increase the risk of certain cancers, including colorectal cancer,” Thanki continued.

Perelman said the diet used in the study was extremely high in fat, providing 60% of calories from fat, primarily from lard.

“We know that the health effects of dietary fats differ depending on their sources and fatty acid composition, and we cannot assume that a diet rich in unsaturated fats from foods such as olive oil, nuts, and seeds would produce the same effects,” she noted.

Perelman added that the researchers primarily studied stem cells from the small intestine rather than the colon.

“Although these tissues share some biological characteristics, they have important differences, and we cannot assume that the same changes would occur in the colon,” she explained.

Finally, Perelman noted that “the mice consuming the high fat diet also gained weight, making it difficult to separate the effects of dietary fat itself from those associated with increased body fat and other metabolic changes.”

Not all high fat foods are unhealthy. Some high fat foods that offer healthy benefits include:

“The goal shouldn’t necessarily be to eliminate fat because our bodies need healthy fats for normal functioning,” said Thanki. “Instead, people should focus on reducing saturated and trans fats while incorporating healthier unsaturated fats in appropriate amounts.”

Thanki had several dietary suggestions. Among them:

“Consistency matters more than perfection. Even modest dietary changes, maintained over time, can have meaningful health benefits,” Thanki said.

Perelman agreed. “The overall quality of the diet matters. Diets that are very high in fat may displace other foods and nutrients that have important health benefits,” she said.

“For colorectal cancer specifically, higher intakes of dietary fiber and whole grains have consistently been associated with lower risk,” Perelman noted.

“Therefore, rather than focusing exclusively on reducing fat, it is important to consider the overall dietary pattern and whether it provides adequate amounts of fiber-rich plant foods and other beneficial nutrients.”

Kirkpatrick agreed that the type of fat is what is important. “A Mediterranean-style diet that includes olive oil, nuts, seeds, and fatty fish can be relatively high in fat but is consistently associated with positive health outcomes,” she said.

“On the other hand, a diet high in saturated fat, processed meats, and ultra-processed foods may increase the risk of certain chronic diseases, particularly when it’s also low in fiber and other protective nutrients.”

Kirkpatrick also noted that many lifestyle habits contribute to a person’s overall health.

“Our health is influenced by so many factors beyond diet, including how we manage stress, our sleep habits, physical activity, genetics, and overall lifestyle,” she said.

Usher and Chris Brown Cancel Additional Shows on R&B Tour

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

More shows have been removed from the schedule on Chris Brown and Usher‘s co-headlining R&B tour. On Nov. 11, upcoming shows in Los Angeles at SoFi Stadium on Nov. 16 and Hard Rock Stadium in Miami on Dec. 6 were marked on the official Ticketmaster website as having been canceled.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

New details have emerged regarding the arrest of Thomas Markle, Meghan Markle’s father, on charges of possessing child pornography. According to People magazine, Doria Ragland, mother of the Duchess of Sussex, assisted the FBI and played a key role in identifying her ex-husband, leading to his arrest in Florida on November 6. (Thomas Markle has previously denied any possession of child sex abuse material.)

The Sun also reports that she played a central role, one that, as the outlet writes, involved reviewing a series of photographs obtained by federal agents. Ragland was reportedly shown a series of blurred images depicting alleged sexual abuse of minors, and she identified the man in the photographs as her ex-husband. Markle, now 82, was reportedly between 30 and 40 years old at the time the photos were taken, during a period when he may not have even been married to Ragland yet (they were married from 1979 to 1987, while Meghan was born in 1981). Markle was also reportedly recognized by another person, whose name has not been disclosed. This person is said to be a witness who spent a great deal of time with him around the time the photos were allegedly taken.

The photographs in question were found among the material seized from a storage unit that Markle rented in Los Angeles. After a court appearance in Tampa following his arrest, he was released on the condition that he remain at the Maravida Senior Living nursing home in Lakeland, Florida—the same facility from which he had been taken into custody.

Meanwhile, following Ragland’s intervention, Meghan is also reportedly willing to cooperate with the FBI in the investigation into her father, a source told The Sunday Times. The Duchess of Sussex no longer has any relationship with her father, and—as was revealed when the story about the alleged child pornography surfaced—it is precisely for this reason. “I hope it can now be understood that, despite certain media publications’ distortion over the years, any distance from my father, and my choice to not allow him to have contact with our children, was for one reason: to protect them,” Meghan said in a statement on the matter. “I trusted that the authorities were doing the same for other families.” She continued: “Although the breadth of what has now been uncovered is more sickening than one could ever imagine, my sincerest hope is that justice will be fully served and that the priority remains the safety and protection of innocent children.” This is a story with many unresolved issues. More will be known after the first hearing, the date of which has not yet been set.

Scientists Discover a Hidden Weakness in One of the Deadliest Cancers

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Researchers have identified another potential approach to treating pancreatic cancer, with early experiments suggesting that blocking a protein could slow tumor growth and strengthen immune defenses.

Pancreatic tumors can be surrounded by tissue so dense that it compresses blood vessels, making it difficult for nutrients to reach cancer cells. Even under these conditions, the cells continue growing. How they obtain the materials needed to survive has remained an important question.

Researchers at Fox Chase Cancer Center have identified one way pancreatic cancer cells obtain cholesterol and other fats, known as lipids. Their study, published in Nature Cancer, also identified a protein that helps control this process and could offer a potential target for cancer treatment.

The research focused on pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC), the most common type of pancreatic cancer. These tumors contain large numbers of cancer-associated fibroblasts (CAFs), cells that produce and organize connective tissue. As CAFs and their collagen fibers expand, they can compress nearby blood vessels, restricting the tumor’s blood supply.

Despite contributing to this nutrient shortage, CAFs also help cancer cells overcome it.

“We found that CAFs can act as a source of these nutrients,” said Edna Cukierman, PhD, a professor and co-leader of the Cancer Signaling and Microenvironment Program at Fox Chase and co-senior author of the study. “Using laboratory and animal models, we discovered that CAF units acquire cholesterol from the bloodstream and transfer it to nearby pancreatic cancer cells through a process called trogocytosis”

Trogocytosis occurs when one cell takes small pieces of another cell’s membrane during direct contact. In this case, pancreatic cancer cells acquire pieces of CAF membranes containing cholesterol and other lipids that support their survival and growth.

The researchers discovered that this transfer depends on a protein called anoctamin 6 (ANO6), which helps prepare the CAF membrane for the exchange.

“These findings help explain how pancreatic cancer cells obtain essential nutrients despite the unusual structure of the pancreatic tumor microenvironment,” said Igor Astsaturov, MD, PhD, a professor at Fox Chase and co-senior author of the study. “Cancer-associated fibroblasts are not passive bystanders. They actively support tumor growth by transferring lipids to cancer cells.”

When researchers reduced or eliminated ANO6 in CAFs, the cells became less effective at supporting pancreatic cancer cell survival. In mouse models, tumors supported by CAFs with functional ANO6 grew substantially faster than those without functional ANO6.

The same nutrient transfer process also appeared to interfere with the immune system.

Cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) are immune cells that recognize and kill cancer cells. When these T cells come into contact with CAFs, they can acquire membrane material containing cholesterol. Excess cholesterol in the tumor environment can contribute to T-cell exhaustion, making these immune cells less effective at fighting cancer.

“With Serge Fuchs, professor at the University of Pennsylvania and co-author on the paper, we were surprised to find that the same cellular mechanism can benefit the tumor in two ways – by supplying cancer cells with lipids while also weakening cancer-fighting T cells,” said Astsaturov. “Targeting ANO6 could potentially disrupt both of these tumor-supporting functions at the same time.”

In mouse models, blocking ANO6 reduced cholesterol accumulation in T cells within tumors and improved markers of their activity. Laboratory experiments also showed that T cells exposed to CAFs with reduced ANO6 were better able to kill cancer cells.

The researchers then tested whether drugs that interfere with ANO6-dependent activity could slow tumor growth. In mouse models, treatment with niclosamide or clofazimine significantly reduced tumor weight. Niclosamide also reduced cholesterol delivery to pancreatic cancer cells.

These findings suggest that targeting ANO6 could both restrict a nutrient source for pancreatic tumors and improve the immune response against them. However, the results remain preclinical, and further research is needed to determine whether selectively blocking ANO6 would work as a treatment in patients.

Trogocytosis is also only one of several ways pancreatic cancer cells may obtain lipids. The researchers suggest that the drugs tested could potentially help treat other difficult cancers, although that possibility requires further investigation.

“We believe our findings identify ANO6 as a potential therapeutic target that could simultaneously disrupt the nutrient supply supporting pancreatic cancer cells and reduce the immune-suppressive effects of the tumor microenvironment,” said Astsaturov.

The findings also build on nearly a decade of research into how the tissue surrounding pancreatic tumors supports cancer growth.

“Excitingly, reducing ANO6 expression also disrupted the stromal synapse-mimicry circuit we previously identified, which coordinates tumor nutritional support, CAF-unit metabolism, and immune suppression. These findings position ANO6 as the missing mechanistic link that unifies nearly a decade of discoveries by our team,” said Cukierman.

Reference: “ANO6-mediated trogocytosis of cancer-associated fibroblasts promotes pancreatic cancer cell growth and immune suppression” by Charline Ogier, Akino Mercy Charles Solomon, Elena Bondarenko, Zhijing Wu, Zhen Lu, Quoc Thang Bui, Mayura R. Dhamdhere, Yingying Tang, Ludmila Recoules, Alena Klochkova, Linara Gabitova-Cornell, Battuya Bayarmagnai, Diana Restifo, Aizhan Surumbayeva, Débora B. Vendramini-Costa, Alexander Y. Deneka, Ralph Francescone, Anna C. Lilly, Alyssa Sipman, Jaye C. Gardiner, Tiffany Luong, Janusz Franco-Barraza, Aleksandr Dolskii, Mariia Dmitrieva, Kathy Q. Cai, Margret B. Einarson, Emmanuelle Nicolas, Andrei Efimov, Emily Megill, Nathaniel W. Snyder, Sven Miller, Mark Andrake, Natalie Phinney, Rolf Brekken, Jérôme Cros, Erica A. Golemis, Yunyun Zhou, Bojana Gligorijevic, Corinne Bousquet, Jonathan Soboloff, Serge Y. Fuchs, Edna Cukierman and Igor Astsaturov, 30 October 2026, Nature Cancer. DOI: 10.1038/s43018-026-01265-5

This research was supported by the National Institutes of Health, the Department of War, the American Cancer Society, the Pew Charitable Fund, the 5th AHEPA Cancer Research Foundation, the Pancreatic Cancer Cure Foundation, Mrs. Marina P. Zazanis and the Zazanis family, Jeffrey and Korey Bernstein, and the M&C Pancreatic Cancer Institute at Fox Chase Cancer Center.

BJC Health, St. Luke's reveal plans to merge" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Missouri-based BJC Health and St. Luke's Hospital are planning a merger.

The providers announced on Wednesday that they have signed a letter of intent to come together, bringing the community hospital into the fold with the Show Me State's largest health system. The pair expects the deal to improve access and improve local care in the St. Louis region.

St. Luke's said in the announcement that the letter "follows a deliberate and thorough evaluation process," conducted by its board of directors and leaders "to identify the best path forward for St. Luke’s and the communities it serves."

The providers said they intend to spend the next several months building a definitive agreement to merge and complete the needed regulatory approval process. In the interim, St. Luke's patients can continue to see their regular physicians.

BJC said in the announcement that it is committed to supporting local, community care while also allowing St. Luke's to tap into the resources available across its ecosystem. It intends to provide key support for the hospital's clinical technology and equipment as well as backing infrastructure and facility upgrades and provider recruitment and retention efforts.

St. Luke’s is a well-respected hospital, known for clinical quality, patient experience and the personal attention given to everyone in our care,” said Andy Bagnall, CEO of St. Luke’s, in the announcement. “At the same time, the demands of health care delivery and our communities’ needs are changing rapidly."

We believe that together with BJC, we can strengthen patient care in St. Louis in ways we could not easily do on our own," Bagnall said.

Nick Barto, CEO of BJC Health, called St. Luke's "a trusted leader in our region with a reputation for excellence."

"Together, we will build on that foundation through our aligned clinical strengths and complementary services," Barto said in the announcement. "Our shared goal is to expand access to differentiated, high-quality care while ensuring St. Luke’s can continue serving the growing communities of West St. Louis for generations to come.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/carelon-agrees-pay-10m-settle-ghost-network-coverage-denial-allegations-new-york" hreflang="en">Carelon agrees to pay $10M to settle ghost network, coverage denial allegations in New York</a>

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Carelon agrees to pay $10M to settle ghost network, coverage denial allegations in New York" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Carelon will pay more than $10 million across two settlements in New York over mental health networks and coverage denials.

The company, a division of Elevance Health, will pay $2.35 million in penalties, fees and costs as well as up to $8 million in restitution for members who paid out-of-pocket for out-of-network mental healthcare, to reimburse those whose claims were improperly denied or to overhaul its networks.

New York Attorney General Letitia James said in an announcement that the settlements resolve two investigations from her office that determined the company misled members by directing them to seek care from providers that were out-of-network—through so-called "ghost networks"—and improperly denied coverage for treatment for substance abuse disorder.

James noted that Carelon administers behavioral health benefits for multiple plans, including Anthem Blue Cross, EmblemHealth, Independent Health and the Empire Plan, which covers state and local government workers.

Across these insurers, Carelon manages behavioral health benefits for more than 4 million people in the state, James said.

The alleged behavior also goes against a 2015 agreement with the AG's office, where the company agreed that it would maintain accurate provider networks and "use approved criteria" when denying substance abuse treatments.

"When someone is ready to seek help for a mental health condition or addiction, every day matters," said James. “Carelon left New Yorkers calling provider after provider who could not see them and made it harder for people to get the treatment to which they were entitled."

"These settlements will help people get the care they need and put money back in New Yorkers’ pockets," she said.

Elevance Health had not responded to a request for comment at the time of publication.

Carelon was hit with a lawsuit in April over the alleged ghost networks and their impact on the Empire Plan in particular. In the case, plaintiff's attorneys conducted secret shopper calls to analyze Carelon's network, calling 300 providers. Just 17% of those contacted took insurance and were open to new patients.

These "ghost networks" have also drawn concern in other markets, such as for maternal care services in Medicaid and in Medicare Advantage. A study released last month found that commercial plans also routinely have inaccurate provider directories.

1 in 3 Infants Diagnosed With Peanut Allergy May Not Have the Condition

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Peanut allergies have been on the rise in recent decades.

According to statistics provided by Food Allergy Research & Education (FARE), an estimated 6.2 million children and adults in the United States are allergic to peanuts.

However, new research published in the November 2026 issue of The Journal of Allergy and Clinical Immunology suggests that over a third of infants thought to have this diagnosis might not actually have a peanut allergy.

In fact, having a history of reacting to peanuts and positive allergy testing on their own may not be sufficient to diagnose a peanut allergy for many infants.

The study’s authors suggest that a threshold oral food challenge — in which the child is given increasing amounts of the suspected food allergen under medical supervision to look for an allergic reaction — should be routinely considered to help confirm peanut allergy.

This additional testing could safely prevent children who are not allergic to peanuts from undergoing unnecessary treatment with oral immunotherapy.

It could also spare families from burdensome food restrictions and allergy precautions.

The researchers’ goal was to evaluate the accuracy and safety of the Allergy Development to an Accelerated Path to Tolerance (ADAPT) peanut oral immunotherapy program, which was established in 2024 at 10 public children’s hospitals across Australia.

This program provides a standardized approach to evaluating and treating infants with suspected peanut allergies.

The study included infants who had experienced a mild to moderate reaction to peanut before 12 months of age and had evidence of peanut sensitization through allergy testing.

Researchers reviewed medical records to examine how accurately the program’s approach identified peanut allergies and whether it could safely guide treatment.

A key part of the evaluation was a threshold oral food challenge. The challenge involved up to seven doses, ranging from 15 to 1,300 milligrams of peanut protein.

Medical teams monitored the infants for reactions and provided treatment when necessary.

Researchers reviewed records from April 2024 through October 2025. During that period, 1,275 infants were assessed through the program, and 778 underwent an oral food challenge.

The researchers also examined allergy test results and whether infants who reacted during the challenges could begin oral immunotherapy at an appropriate starting dose.

At the time of the oral food challenge, the infants had a median age of 13 months.

Of the 778 infants who underwent an oral food challenge, 507 (65.2%) had an allergic reaction.

However, 271 (34.8%) did not react to peanut during the supervised test, despite having a history of a previous reaction and positive allergy test results.

These findings suggest that relying on medical history and allergy tests alone may lead some infants to be labeled with a peanut allergy when they can actually tolerate the food.

Among the infants who reacted during the challenges, 61 experienced anaphylaxis, a severe allergic reaction that can affect breathing and blood pressure. This represented 12% of infants with a positive challenge and 7.8% of all infants who underwent testing. Medical teams safely managed these severe allergic reactions.

Researchers also found that infants who reacted to peanut generally had higher skin prick test results and Ara h 2-specific IgE levels than those who did not react.

However, these test results did not reliably indicate how much peanut would trigger a reaction or how severe that reaction would be.

Of the 394 infants who began peanut oral immunotherapy, 384 (97.5%) successfully started treatment at a dose below the amount that had triggered a reaction during their food challenge.

The researchers say these findings support considering supervised oral food challenges to confirm suspected peanut allergies before starting treatment or recommending long-term avoidance.

Erin Banta, MD, a board certified allergist and immunologist who treats both children and adults at Latitude Food Allergy Care in Great Neck, New York, said these findings are significant because they provide evidence for early use of oral food challenges in infants with peanut reactions.

“In those with mild-moderate prior reactions to peanut, early oral food challenge may allow for either delabeling of food allergy (in 1/3 of the patients in this study) or diagnosis of food allergy,” she told Healthline.

Banta, who wasn’t involved in the study, added that early delabeling takes a significant burden off the patient and their family.

“On the other hand,” she explained, “early diagnosis allows for early initiation of treatment with oral immunotherapy at a time in the patient’s life associated with the greatest success rates.”

Banta further noted that the study offers different pathways for oral immunotherapy based on the patient’s reaction threshold. This means that some patients may be able to complete treatment more quickly due to their individual threshold dose.

Lindsey Forstie, FNP, a family nurse practitioner with clinical training and experience in allergy and asthma care at Carefree Allergy in Phoenix, AZ, added that while further evaluation may be needed to confirm a peanut allergy diagnosis, it’s important for parents not to try introducing peanuts at home.

“Instead, they should speak with their pediatrician or allergist about appropriate testing and whether a supervised food challenge is safe and necessary,” she said.

According to Forstie, who was also not part of the study, the goal is to avoid unnecessary food restrictions while also keeping children safe.

“The key message is that not every positive allergy test means a child has a food allergy,” she told Healthline. “Accurate diagnosis helps protect children while allowing those who are not allergic to enjoy a more normal, less restrictive diet.”

The 5 Best Noah Wyle ‘ER’ Episodes of the '90s, Ranked

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

pNBC began airing ER in 1994, and the show quickly became one of the most believable medical dramas of all time. A reason behind this is the beloved character Dr. John Carter, portrayed by Noah Wyle. Noah's character became one of the best television doctors, and critics noticed his uniqueness by the end of Season 1.

Noah received a string of Emmy and Golden Globe nominations for his portrayal, along with four consecutive Actor Awards. He starred on the show for 11 seasons and returned for other story arcs from start to finish, yet his performances were not enough for award recognition after 1999. Here's a list of Dr. Carter's best ER episodes of the '90s, with stories that made this beloved character prove he belongs in an emergency room.

Every County General employee needs a close friend, and for a short time, Carter's was fellow surgical intern Dr. Dennis Gant (Omar Epps). The pair were prepared to become the best surgeons they could be, and stuck by each other when doing so. Their behavior toward one another began to change as time went on, and it made Carter unable to see the signs Gant was struggling. It wasn't until Gant was struck and killed by a train in the episode prior that Carter realized he didn't see what was right in front of him.

Gant's death was one of the most tension-filled times in Carter's life as he couldn't help but wonder if his late friend jumped in front of the train. The survivor's guilt was eating at Carter, and the lack of care he had when expressing his anger toward co-workers was surprising to see so early in his career. The police ultimately ruled Gant's death an accident, and it made doctors a little bit at ease. The cause of death didn't change what Carter felt, and it was stunning to see him question his behavior as a friend, and not as a fellow intern.

The family lives of doctors have been woven into plot lines before, with Carter's being known for their intensity and privileged background. He did maintain positive relationships with some of his relatives, including his cousin, Chase (Jonathan Scarfe). Carter was shocked to learn his cousin was a heroin addict, but he believed that as a doctor and close relative, he could help him get clean. He put in the time and effort with Dr. Anna Del Amico (Maria Bellow), and thought it worked. Carter's plan backfired, and Chase landed in the hospital from a heroin overdose.

Despite Chase's history, Carter's shock was felt throughout the entire hospital room as he tried to resuscitate his cousin. That shock turned into fear while admitting to his grandparents that he was aware of Chase's addiction, and did not tell them about it. Carter had longed for unconditional love from many family members, yet he always had it from Chase. Chase suffered permanent brain damage from his overdose, but Carter sleeping next to his bedside was enough to know that neurological issues would not make him love his cousin any less.

A normal day at County General turned into chaos following a chemical spill. Employees and patients were unsure of what to do, and Dr. Kerry Weaver (Laura Innes) being exposed to the fumes only made things crazier. Carter put his panic aside, and took charge of the ER without hesitation. He directed staff and patients to rooms based on health status while conducting research and communicating with emergency officials.

Carter brought intensity to a whole new level after he was put in charge, and it showed how much he'd grown as a medical professional. He gained more self-confidence just by doing his job, and didn't even realize it until he knew everyone was safe. Carter's work and determination saved hundreds of lives, but he did take a short pause from his leadership duties after finding out his elderly patient had died. Carter snapped out of commander mode when Weaver recovered from her fume exposure and smiled at the employees praising him, realizing that County General's ER was his home.

It's hard to imagine Carter practicing something other than emergency medicine. After figuring that out himself, Carter fought tooth and nail to convince his superior, Dr. Donald Anspaugh (John Aylward) of the same thing. Anspaugh initially refused due to budgetary reasons and Carter signed a contract to complete the County General's surgical program. He changed his mind once Carter explained why he would never be a great surgeon.

It was tough to predict Anspaugh's final decision. He and Carter's reasons behind their opinions of a specialty change were understandable. Carter obviously wasn't backing down, and even if it meant leaving his surgical mentor, Dr. Peter Benton (Eriq La Salle), he couldn't stop fighting for what was right for him. Carter's fight was inspirational for anyone brave enough to follow their hearts, and it led to him becoming one of the best emergency medicine doctors at County General.

Carter was a third-year medical student anxious to become a surgeon on the first episode of ER. He walked in wearing a "tailored white coat," looking nervous and terrified. Carter was eager to begin treating patients, even though he never learned how to start an IV. Something he wasn't afraid of doing when he walked in was interacting with patients, and threw up outside after doing so.

Carter walked back into the ER with his head held high after talking with Dr. Mark Greene (Anthony Edwards). He continued to be imperfect, but he didn't let that get into his head. It was beautiful to see a young student believing in himself on the first day working in a Chicago hospital ER. That beauty is what helped him become a phenomenal doctor who saved millions of lives worldwide.

Warhammer Survivors Review: A Brutal New Bullet Heaven Game

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Warhammer Survivors is a brutal spin-off of Vampire Survivors, pitting heroes from Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar against endless hordes of enemies while maintaining the addictive gameplay loop.

Few games have a title as accurate or descriptive to its core content as Warhammer Survivors. In a nutshell, itt’s Vampire Survivors gameplay, but with Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar characters, monsters, and levels, but with the difficulty turned up to eleven.

Well, that’s a bit of an unfair introduction. If Warhammer Survivors was just a cosmetic upgrade, they would have just released it as DLC in Vampire Survivors, rather than giving it its own dedicated game. After all, Vampire Survivors has invited Castlevania to its roster and added Contra too, so surely Space Marines wouldn’t look out of place?

Thankfully, Warhammer Survivors shakes up the gameplay and adds new tweaks to the familiar formula that makes it a worthwhile purchase for people who aren’t familiar with the tabletop game. Long-time Vampire Survivors fans just need to be aware that its Warhammer equivalent is a fair bit harder than the content they’ve beaten before.

At its core, Warhammer Survivors has the same gameplay loop as Vampire Survivors and its many clones. The player picks a hero, who then has to survive against endless hordes of enemies. The main gameplay gimmick is that the player cannot control how the character shoots: just how they move across the battlefield.

Throughout each level, the player earns experience points from killing enemies, and when they level up, they are given new weapons and upgrades that make it easier to destroy more foes. As time goes on, the enemies get stronger and grow in numbers, meaning careful movement becomes even more important.

There are also bosses that appear throughout the stage, which are outlined in a neon green aura. Slaying these bosses results in crates being dropped with level upgrades. These are important, as if a weapon is sufficiently levelled, then a crate could carry its improved version, such as the Boltgun transforming into the Heavy Boltgun, which has a drastically improved fire rate.

Dying on a run isn’t the end of the world, as the player can find coins strewn throughout each stage that can be spent on static buffs and upgrades. These make future runs easier, so the player has a greater chance of surviving later stages.

Warhammer Survivors keeps this gameplay loop intact, though it splits everything across two worlds. On the main menu, the player selects the “Universe”: Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar, the latter of which is the modern replacement for Warhammer Fantasy (RIP). They have their own stages, characters, and enemies, with no direct crossovers between the two.

Technically, there is content that connects the two, as the upgrades unlocked are shared between the two, meaning working hard in one helps out the other. There are also non-franchise-specific levels that they share, like the Hobby Table, but for the most part, the Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar parts of the game are separate.

A new indie inspired by Vampire Survivors tasks players with defending their home computer from an onslaught of 2000s-era viruses.

So, if Warhammer Survivors is just Vampire Survivors with Ork Boyz, then is it worth buying? Well, there are some key differences that justify it existing as its own game, rather than DLC for Vampire Survivors.

In Vampire Survivors, all the weapons are tied to the player character. Warhammer Survivors changes this with its Follower system, as characters are now unlocked alongside their weapon when the player levels up. This means they follow the selected hero around, and their attacks will originate from them.

The Follower system might not seem like a big addition, but needing to account for the changing starting position of attacks makes the gameplay considerably more challenging. This is especially true for the Garlic-type weapon users, which have a static area-of-effect centred on their hero. In Warhammer Survivors, the party members move around after the player, meaning the player has to guide them, like a kite, into the enemy hordes.

The other big difference in Warhammer Survivors is how it handles end of stage bosses. In Vampire Survivors, if the player spends too long in a stage, they’ll eventually be attacked by the Grim Reaper and quickly killed. Warhammer Survivors replaces the manifestation of death with the Extremis Bosses: foes that appear at the twenty-five minute mark for an epic fight.

The Follower system might not seem like a big addition, but needing to account for the changing starting position of attacks makes the gameplay considerably more challenging.

The Extremis Bosses can’t be faced without preparation, however, as the player needs to unlock a specific item in each stage to be able to damage them. If the player doesn’t have this item, then the boss will be invincible, and it will automatically kill the hero in a cutscene.

The enemies the player faces on each stage can also have unique gimmicks, usually tied to their bosses. It’s possible for Tyranids to buff their weaker units, making them considerably faster, while the followers of Nurgle can summon massive poison-spewing chimneys, creating damaging areas for the player to walk through.

The Orks of Warhammer 40,000 easily have the best gimmick, as they can call a mighty “Waaagh”, bringing endless waves of units against the player until it ends. Surviving a Waaagh can be one of the trickiest parts of the Ork stage, unless the player prepares lots of AoE attacks to survive the horde.

The studio behind Vampire Crawlers has revealed how the game came about, thanks to its relationship with the creator of Vampire Survivors.

The new additions to Warhammer Survivors make the game considerably harder than the source game. And make no mistake: I adore Vampire Survivors and have purchased it on multiple platforms. I have endured countless runs with my boy Poe, facing the creatures of the night with nothing but a piece of Garlic and a dream, and came out the other side.

Warhammer Survivors ramps the difficulty up in a few ways. The Follower system means the origin point of your weapons keeps moving around, making it harder to accurately fire at enemies. This isn’t as much of a problem with targeted weapon users, like grenades, but AoE and shooter characters are harder to predict, as they keep moving while shooting.

The changes to bosses are also a point against the game, because Warhammer Survivors does an extremely poor job of conveying exactly what items are needed to be “Prepared” for facing the Extremis Bosses. The best clues the player gets is seeing what items are always available in each stage, as well as blanked out menus that fill out if they happen to use the correct upgrades.

There’s far too little information about what items are needed to face the Extremeis Bosses, especially as the blanked out menu only appears after a twenty-five minute run, which is hard to reach on a good day. Most players aren’t going to bother doing trial and error: they’ll just Google the answers as soon as the game launches, so why bother obfuscating it?

It also bears mentioning that the Age of Sigmar characters are way less useful than their 40,000 equivalents. The Warhammer 40,000 roster includes gun and grenade users, while the ranged Age of Sigmar characters tend to be much slower. Thankfully, the upgrades menu stays the same between rosters, so it’s possible to grind with the Warhammer 40,000 cast for stat upgrades, in order to help out their fantasy equivalents.

There are also times when the game will just kill the player with no recourse. The Age of SIgmar stages are particularly bad for this, with enemy cavalry units randomly appearing and rushing across the stage, pushing the player into a wall and instantly killing them in a way that’s impossible to avoid.

The sub bosses fought in each stage also have far, far too much health. I’ve sat there for what felt like an eternity with maxed out weapons, stunlocking a boss into place, while damage numbers just rack up, and they won’t die. It’d be fine if their gameplay made them challenging foes to match their mighty health bar, but many of them are just regular enemies with bigger sprites.

The changes to Warhammer Survivors do provide an extra layer of challenge for those coming from the source material, and I’d be lying if I said I didn’t enjoy the new additions and wrinkles: it just needs a few more tweaks and balance checks for the game to be as good as it can be.

This review covers Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun 2 and why players should play it.

The Warhammer franchise has a reputation for being extremely dark in tone, especially 40,000. The Imperium of Man isn’t exactly a lovely place to exist, between the constant threat of Xeno invasion, the corrupting influence of Chaos present in the souls of mankind, and the rampant corruption within society itself.

Warhammer Survivors is a rare example of Warhammer being tongue-in-cheek and referential. This can be seen through its many nods to the actual game and its history, especially the weapons, such as exploding d6s hitting the board, Nuln Oil slowing foes down, the measuring tape increasing damage AoE, and the experience point booster being called White Dwarf.

The highlight of this approach is the Hobby Table stage, where the player fights off monsters on a crafting table, the kind used by many real-life fans of the tabletop game. The developers had mentioned in the past that the Hobby Table stage is a nod to Micro Machines, and it shows, being easily the most fun level in Warhammer Survivors.

The visuals and overall aesthetic of Warhammer Survivors are close to that of Vampire Survivors, and like the Boltgun series, the approach is mired in retro goodness. Few Warhammer games have managed to cram as many foes onto the screen as this one, making Warhammer Survivors one of the few titles to ever capture the essence of the beloved artwork from the series, save shown through a 16-bit filter.

Warhammer Survivors doesn’t try to tell a story: it’s about throwing iconic heroes from the franchise against familiar foes. It’s clear the people who made the game are huge fans of the source material, and that love shines throughout the experience.

The new layer of difficulty in Warhammer Survivors can be frustrating at times, but it also feels necessary for helping distinguish it from Vampire Survivors. If the player can soldier through the hard moments, then they’ll get another massive dose of Survivors gameplay and content, which is likely a huge selling point on its own for people who are still addicted to the original.

Warahmmer Survivors justifies itself as a separate entity throughout plenty of new characters and stages, with enough gameplay gimmicks to ensure it’s not just a reskin. The difficulty can be frustrating at times, but people don’t sign up for a Survivors-like to have an easy time. Warhammer Survivors will satisfy people looking for more of the familiar but different enough to not be a rehash, while still paying tribute to the universes that won over fans worldwide.

2026's best game so far is finally coming to more platforms, letting mobile audiences become as hopelessly addicted as console and PC players.
